The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
S i r:
I have the honor to inform the Department that
through the good offices of a local American a young
Spaniard by the name of J. Salvador Sanchez of Calle
Bravo 799, telephone 31262, Lima, called at the
Confidential File
Embassy yesterday to communicate information he be-
lieves will be interest to our Government regarding
the activity in Peru of certain Japanese whom Mr.
Sanchez believes to be operating under th vorders
CLLLA
MAR13
of the Japanese Government.
Mr.

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Mr. Sanchez has a parently long been a resident
in Poru and has at vurious times been the proprietor
and manager of siall hotels at various places in the
northern part of Peru, particularly Pacasmayo, Huaráz
and Huacho. Hr. Sanchez said that his attention be-
gan to be excited by the activity of certain Japanese
as early as 1918 and that as nearly as he can make out
the plan is for the Jupanese to place in Mexico, Brazil
and Peru large contingents of men, who are really
Japanese soldiers, so that in case of trouble, they
can be rapidly massed at sono strategic point, be
picked up by transports and be used where needed in
any attack upon Aerican lines of communication, par-
ticularly the Panana Canal, and American territory.
Mr. Sanchez says that there has been a steady
infiltration o Japanese both regular and clandestine
into Peru for years. As t e Department knows, it is
notorious that both Chinese and Japanese in large
numbers have been coming into Peru in spite of the
Peruvian Imnigration Laws through the connivance of
various Peruvian officials, who made money out of
the traffic. The Japanese  inister himself has told
me that his colony numbered some 30,oo0. But of course
it is not to be immediately deduced from this that
any considerable number of the colony are ex-soldiers
under the control of the Japanese Government waiting
for a moment to attack the United States.
Mr. Sanchez believes, however, that Japan has
roughly

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
roughly 15,0o0 men in Peru would could be mobilized at
ports along the coast within very short order. He
states that the Japanese who have been coming into
the country, many of whom have stopped at his hotel,
have all had money and that they have consistently
bought and developed properties, saved their profits,
reinvested in other properties and have extended their hol-
dings. This would seem like legitimate activity, but
Mr. Sanchez is convinced that these properties are
strategically located and that their ultimate purpose
is to support some military manoeuver on the part of
the Japanese Government. He states that they are oc-
casionally inspected by the Japanese Minister. He also
states that he has seen a large map issued by the Jap-
anese Government, which was in the possession of one
of the men who once stopped at his hotel, showing the
location of the Japanese reserves in Peru an mobiliza-
tion plans.
A number of years ago Mr. Sanchez says
certain Teixo Nishi (or Nichi), a Japanese captain of
frigate, came to Peru and initiated the present plan.
He describes him as being a man of evident culture and
training who wore a short black beard, who had cards
showing his official title, who had lots of money and
who bought a nu ber of properties. He states that this
man after being in the country for several years told
him he had been called home by his Government.
Operating under Nichi was a certain Kurikawa, who
seems

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
seems to have been the chief buyer of properties. This
man has bought constantly and only recently purchased,
according to Mr. Sanchez, the Hacienda of Paramonga and
another property called Supe. He states that still a
further Japanese by the name of Madsuda has purchased
a large hacienda named Palpa in the vicinity of Huaral,
and that finally here in Lima there is an Army Captain
by tie name of Nakamura, who is the owner of a small
grocer: shop in the Calle de Bravo. Of course there
are a number of apanese merchants in Lima and Japanese
artesans and small shopkeepers abound. Mr. Sanchez is
convinced, ho ever, that this Army Captain Nakamura is
engaged on te official usiness of his country and
states that he and Kurikawa have returned to Japan
within the last two months.
Mr. Sanchez spoke of another map he had seen
showing the Pacific Ocean and indicating plans for
the capture of the Philippines and the Hawaiian Islands,
as well as defense plans for Japan proper.
The Department has doubtless received a great
many stories along the same lines as that of Mr. Sanchez.
I remember that they were current in Mexico and have ex-
isted ever since the Russo-Japanese War. I also recall
that I once, when stationed at the Department, made a
thorugh investigation of the Magdalena Bay situation
only to find that a California land promoter, named
Jackling, was engaged in an effort to cause someone to
relieve him of some lands in that vicinity and had been
inventing most of the scare stories. Nevertheless at
a time like the present I felt I should pass on to the
Department

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.93)
-5-
Department the story which hes come to me, as it was
sincerely related by the young Spaniard who was brought
to the Embassy, wi.ose chief idea was that there would
be an attack on tie Panama Canal and that men stationed
in Peru would be used in connection with it. I shall
be glaa to endeavor to secure additional data in case
our ar or Navy Department believes it necessary.
respestfully yours,
Fred Morris Dearing.

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Embassy's Despatch No. 1709, April 15,1932.
Lima, April 1l, 1932.
MEMORANDUM
For the Ambassador
From Mr. Ackerson
Mr. Gildred called on Friday last at yaur request
and brought with him Señor Sanchez, with whom you had an
interview on Feb. 5, 1932, regarding the supposed ac-
tivities of the Japanese in Central and South America.
As you were unable to see these gentlemen, I interviewed
them and wish to report the new information which Señor
Sanchez was able to give.
Señor Sanchez stressed again the fact that large
numbers of Japanese are immigrating into Peru, Mexico,
Panama and other Central and South American countries.
He says that the Japanese agents, who arrange for this im-
migration, pay to the governments or to the officials of
these governments a sum of money amounting to about $looo
per person permitted to enter. In Peru it appears that
one man is entered under this arrangement and then some
twenty or thirty other persons are subsequently brought
here to join him who are reputed to be relatives or other
members of his family. These people are placed on haciendas
which have already been purchased by Japanese army officers
and these distributed nuolei of workers form the basis for
what will eventually become a Japanese military force.
Señor Sanohez states that a very large number of Japanese
are entering these countries every year and particularly
at the present time, all of them being brought, of course,
by the Japansse boats which come direct to these shores
from

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
from Japan. However, he did not make any estimate of
the number of immigrants and did not intimate that he
had any first-hand knowledge that the number was in-
creasing, but apparently he was repeating the statements
which his Japanese friends had made.
The Panama Canal appears to be one of the key points
upon which the military attack upon the United States, if
it should become necessary, will concentrate. These
Japanese army officers claim to have devised a plan
1
whereby the Canal can be rendered useless for a consider-
able time by the loss of only one single life. Whether
this means the loss of one single Japanese life or the loss
of one American life, I am not sure, but I rather gathered
that not more than one person would be sacrificed in this
attempt. The scheme provides, amongst other things, for
the return of Panama to Colombia. If this is so, it should
be possible to get some information from that country with
regard to any such activities which the Japanese may have
started. Other details of the plan promise financial in-
dependence to Mexico, Central and South America and even
Canada, that is, freedom from their obligations to the
United States and to England. Onoe these countries had
joined foroes with Japan and a revolution or war had been
carried to a successful conclusion, Japan would notify the
countries which had assisted her that they would no longer
have to make payments to their former creditors.
The Japanese informants of Señor Sanchez also state
.that their country has planned to have a world war break
out

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
out during the current year, 1932. An attempt will be
made, of course, to place the blame for this outbreak,
by diplomatic or other means, upon the United States,
England or some other country than Japan. They state that
Japan is well prepared to carry on such a war for six
years, but they expect that its purpose would have been
accomplished in two and a half years and that Japan will
have been the successful one. The Philippines are to be
baken in six days, the Panama Canal is to be destroyed
in very little time and the other military operations
are to be carried out with the greatest possible speed
in order to prevent mobilization by the other countries
of their forces in the Pacific.
The whole scheme apparently is aimed particularly
against the United States and England. The Japanese
officers even said that much of the present unrest and
agitation in India was due to Japanese propaganda and
that a tremendous amount of money was being spent in India
with a view to alienating it from the mother country.
Señor Sanchez also thought that propaganda would be carried
on in Canada but he was not very specific on this point.
From the point of view of the United States, he thought
that Mexico, Panama and the countries of northern South
America were the vulnerable points.
In this connection, the Japanese officers explain in
a rather interesting manner the apparent failure of their
very superior foroes to overcome the Chinese in and around
Shanghai with greater despatch. They say that this is
merely

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.98)
一4 -
merely a blind to "prove" to the rest of the world
that Japan is really not prepared to carry on a war of
large proportions and thus to lull the other countries
of the world into a false sense of security which will
make easier the carrying out of the plans whioh the
Japanese have formulated. The Japanese officers state
emphatically that the Japanese army is of such power and
organization and is so well supplied with ammunition and
other military requirements that they could wipe out the
Chinese opposition within a very short time if it were
not for this strategic propaganda which their less tenacious
policy is supposed to propagate.
Señor Sanchez states that from his observation these
Japanese officers in Peru are well supplied with money to
carry out their parts in the general plan, and they assure
him that a tremendous amount of money is being expended
particularly in Mexico, Panama and India.
With regard to war machinery, they state they have
invented an antidote to gas, which makes this weapon en-
tirely ineffectual. They are also supposed to have in-
vented some sort of bomb which carries a freezing ch emical
which reduces the temperature of a considerable area around
the point where the bomb bursts to a very low degree.
Señor Sanohez has given us some names of people who
have supplied him with this information and he has also
given their addresses so that it may be possible to check
up on what he has said. The following are the names and
addresses:

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Kurikawa - Hacienda Paramonga
Luis Zoppu - Taller de Carpinteria - Santa Rosa
de los Monjas frente a Guinochio
Nakamura - Bravo, Esq. Pasaje Bravo
BJ
G.G.A.Jr.
P.S. I am trying to get immigration figures for Peru
in order to see whether or not they support Señor
Sanohez' contention. Of course it may be that these
people are being brought in secretly and that therefore
their arrival will not appear in the statistics, but I
hardly think that this would be the case. The preliminary
figures which have been shown to me by the Assistant
Commeroial Attaché indicate that many more Japanese have
left Peru during the last two years than have entered,
but it would not be safe to make a definite statement
until we can get something more acourate.
G.G.A.Jr.

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 3, 1932.
In despatch No. 2250 of October 20,
1932, the Embassy at Lima transmits a
further memorandum on Japanese activities
in Peru against the United States by
Mr. Sanchez, Spanish hotelkeeper of Lima.
Mr. Sanchez does not appear to understand
the Japanese immigration system, which is
fostered, aided and regulated by the Japan-
ese Government, and he is inclined to look
upon the local immigrant leaders, many of
whom are officials of the foreign or Over-
seas Ministries or of immigration societies
in Japan, as secret War Office agents. The
tales Mr. Sanchez alleges to have heard
from Japanese in Peru regarding the designs
of Japanese colonists in South and Central
America, on the Panama Canal, et cetera,
seem like the idle chatter of Japanese ex-
service men living in a "free country offree speech.
The present memorandum by Mr. Sanchez
states that his Japanese friends told him
that the Lima NIPPO, a local Japanese paper,
of October 3-4, published reports from
Tokyo that the Japanese military demand war
with America and that the Japanese nation
and government want an explanation from America
(for the character of the Lytton Report).
The

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.95)
- 2 -
ne issues of the Japanese paper referred
to have been examined carefully but nothing
of the kind is found. The news from Tokyo
relates to the Japanese reaction to the
Lytton Report,with which we are already
familiar. Mr. Sanchez further gathered
from Japanese acquaintances that Japan
wants war with America and will try to bring
it to a head while Japan is prepared and
America unprepared. Sanchez's friends also
inform him that Japanese in American pos-
sessions will assist Japan, and that the
American navy will be defeated because it
is full of foreigners and Manilas (sic).
The warlike frame of mind of Sanchez's
Japanese friends is interesting, but they
may be "pulling his leg".

information from that memorandum is enclosed herewith.

the last page just inside the cover, was given to Señor
Sanchez by a Japanese friend of his, Juan Luis Beppu.
I wish to refer especially to page 3 of my despatch
0,f.
No. 1508, February 6, 1932, in which the names of two

cently, while looking through some old papers, he came

men, Capitan Teizo Nishi and Mr. M. Kurikawa. I enclose
41
herewith these two carās which were left at the Embassy
by Señor Sanchez. It will be seen that the card of
Capitan Nishi states that he is an officer in the Japanese
Navy.

it is unable to vouch for the accoracy of any of this in-
formation given by Señor Sanchez. The Embassy is also at
a 1oss

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.93)

a loss to explain just why he should continue to fur-
nish the Embassy with this information except for the
fact that he was brought here originally by an American
citizen, who, however, is no longer in Lima. It is
felt, however, that is is advisable to pass on this in-
formation to the Department so that it, with its wider
sources of information, may be able to check up and see
whether any importance should be given to what our in-
formant tells us.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No., Embassy Despatch 2250, Oct.20,1932.
Excerpt from Señor Sanchez' Memorandum.
(TRANSLATION)
Although I do not have much time at my disposal,
I nevertheless try to take a certain amount of time
to keeping myself informed of the Japanese activities
against the United States of America.
The Japanese newspaper, Lima Nipo, which is pub-
lished in Lima, is issued every day except Mondays,
since there is no work on Sundays; but on Monday the
third, an extra was published giving an account of
the Lytton report, and, according to what some Japanese
have told me, the newspaper tells that the Japanese
soldiers and marines are asking for war with the United
States, but that the Japanese Government recommends
waiting for a time; but the Army and the Navy insist on
declaring war. The issue of Tuesday the fourth pub-
lished Lytton's picture and the commentary of the
Japanese nation and Government declaring that they are
unanimously agreed in asking the United States for ex-
planations, and that the soldiers and marines want war
and do not want to suffer any more humiliations from
North America. (Newspapers attached herewith). The
general opinion gathered from various members of the
Japanese colony, is that Japan wants war and will
endeavor to bring it to ahead within a year at the
most, since Japan's power is in its full war footing
and the United States, on the contrary, is not well
prepared - its war vessels, armament, aeroplanes, etc.,
are old - and, on the other hand, if war is delayed,
then the danger for Japan will be greater because in
the meantime the United States can modernize its fleet
and better organize its Army and Aviation. It is
further noted that Japan has quite a number of soldiers
in the Philippines and Hawaii, who are only waiting for
armament, that in Hawaii there is a large Japanese colony
and that they have succeeded in having children of
Japanese parents become Hawaiian citizens, and as such
they are under American sovereignty and naturally this
makes it easier for them to carry out their plans - but
that there, as well as in the Philippines, they have
plenty of soldiers and officers who are only awaiting
armament and orders. I asked how they could receive
armament if the American squadron is in the Pacific.
They say that that is of no importance with submarines
and aeroplanes and that the first thing would be an en-
counter of the squadrons and that the Japanese is
superior in vessels and marines, that the American Navy
has too many foreigners and among them there are many
Manilas, and they will not fight against Japan because
Japan is going to free them.
They

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
They say that the Japanese government declares that
it makes no difference if the American squadron is in
the Pacific, because with intelligent maneuvering they
(the Japanese) will conquer, that the necessary thing
to do now is to pacify Manchuria by initiating a very
active campaign, and afterwards instigating a revolution
in the American colonies in the Pacific, thus provoking
war and offering them their independence and support, and
by that means being able to extend themselves territorially
and so have a place to send the excess population which
they have.
A book has been given to me entitled "El Drama Del
Extremo Oriente", published by the "Central Japanese
Society." It is worthy of note that on page 136, at
the end of the article on motives and acts which justify
the intervention, and the place beginning with the words
"Probable complications," is especially marked. I am
sending the book herewith for your information. I am
also sending herewith the cards (which I have had in my
possession since 19l8) of Capitan of Frigate of the
Japanese Navy, Teizo Nishi, and his assistant, M.Kurikawa,
both of whom I met in Hotel Londres de Guadalupe
(Pascamayo) where I was manager, and they went on mission
for their Government to buy or rent lands to bring the
discharged soldiers of their Army, and declaring that they
had left 1500 in Brazil in 1918....

PROLOGO
A LA TRADUCCION CASTELLANA
Hay no pocas obras relatiras a esta cuestión.
famosa qa, en los fastos internaciouales de Extre-

se han publicado articulos de per rlicas, folle-

como gran señor, y celoso de su buen nombre y
de su fama en el mundo, donde desempeña um rol
tan importante, el de puente entre la cirilización
oriental y occidental, ha publicado también obras
de condumio y sumamente razonadas an su fa-
cor: obras en las que ha denostrado que no es
el país absorbente e imperialisia que abusa de
su poder y de sus energías, sino el moderadar y
phasmador de un progreso formidabde que eu-
cierra a Oriente en el molde de una cirilización
comvún al género humano. Pero la mavgor parte
de esas obras están escritas en inglés-l idiona
más común entre los occidentales en Oriente. —
La literatura de este género justificatiro en
otros idioras no es muy abundante: pero on

castellano probablemente por la poca influen-

Por otro lado, el Japón, por su catejoría, de-

solidar la paz, meta suprema e ideal de la civi-

M. Balet ha estudiado las causas del conflic-
to chino-japonés, uno de los problemas canden-
tes del momanto. Es necesario ver el panorama, la
linea evolutiva, los actores y resortes ocultos. Ex-
pone claramente las razones que ha tenido el Ja-
pón para ir a la Manchuria y a la Corea—"necesi-
tado, dice, de romper el circulo de hierro que lo
extrangulaba y obstaculizaba su expansión". Ex-
plica igualmente su acción para dar valor y orga-
nizar estos países.
En los anexos presenta cl texto de los Trata-
dos, acuerdos y protocolos que fijan los derechos
y obligaciones de cada una de las partes.
Todo el proceso se desarrola delante de nues-
tros ojos..
El autor escojita y pone metódicamente en
orden las piezas que lo componen: ha sometido a
la critica y confrontado con sus observaciones
personales, los documentos tomados de los archi-
vos diplomáticos o recogidos en el mismo lugar
de los hechos.
Afirma que el conflicto ha nacido de la vio-
lación de los tratados verificada por la China.
Concluye en favor del Japón; pero se excusa de
pronunciar un alegato: Yo soy, dice, su amigoy
admirador, no su abogado.
Ha escrito un capitulo de vivisimo interés
sobre la Joven China, sus innovadares, sus riva-
lidades interiores y espiritu revolucionario.

¿Tendremos una nuera guerra? Esta es la

les dan las proporciones inquietantes de un con-

Hay personas que se consuelan ante esta
perspectira, exclamando: Ca, las cosas pasan
tan lejos de nosotros!
Palabras necias, como si la guera no fuera

CAPITULO PRIMERO
El asunto del Extremo Oriente:
El teatro y los actores
El conflicto chino-japonés, cuya explicación
es el objeto primordial de este libro. aunque lo-
calizado en un ángulo, la tierra manchú, entre los
dos grandes representantes de la raza amarilla,
toca multitud de problemas, sintetizados. general-
mente, bajo el nombre de “Asunto de Extremo-
Oriente", o también "Problema del Pacifico", cu-
yo conocimiento es indispensable para darse
cuenta del origen, los lineamientos propios para
trazar un plano del teatro, de los actores y de
los hechos anteriores que le dan contornos de
verdadera importancia
El Teatro.—Cuando se habla del Pacífico no
hay que referirse tanto a la inmensidad del océa-
no así llamado, cuanto a los países situados en
sus costas o que emergen de sus aguas: el nom-
bre abraza ambas cosas puesto que los pueblos
situados a sus orillas sírvense del mar que los
baña como de medio de comunicación con los
países vecinos o lejanos; y hacen de sus ondas
el escenario de sus luchas e intereses.

=== Page 42 === (confidence 1.00)
16
EL DRAMA DEL EXTREMO ORIENTE
Entre todos los mares el llamado Pacífico tie-
ne caracteres especiales. Las comarcas que ba-
na con sus ondas son tan varias como las ra-
zas y sub-razas de los pueblos que las habitan.
Alli están representadas todas las civilizaciones.
De la Alaska al Cabo de Hornos; en las orillas
orientales del Pacífico hállanse raros ejempiares
de la raza roja; la raza blanca con dos de sus
más importantes familias, en el Canadá y Estados
Unidos, una amalgama de todas las naciones eu-
ropeas, con el predominio del elemento anglo-
sajón; a partir de Méjico. hasta la Tierra de
Fuego, los latinos, hijos de España y Portugal.
En las orillas occidentales, aparte de los rusos
siberianos sólo se hallan representantes de la ra-
za amarilla: coreanos, chinos, malayos. En medio
de sus aguas "the last but no the least", el Japón;
los innumerables archipiélagos, la Australia, la
Nueva Caledonia, etc., etc. y fragmentos esparci-
dos de un continente dislocado, sin duda, por
cataclismos telúricos de la pre-historia.
Si en el pasado fueron considerados los nma-
res como fosos insondables que separaban a los
pueblos, hay que considerarlos hoy como un lazo
de unión. En esta consideración los ríos son ca-
minos que marchan sin detenerse jamás; los ma-
res, una plataforma de acceso tan cómoda como
la tierra firme.
Existe sólo una diferencia entre los mares
y la tierra; y es que aquellos son una plataforma
móvil y fluida, que no se presta a limitaciones
ni amojonamientos métricos. Libre por su natu-

Los principales aotores

una estación de lluvias. La vegetación recibe de
ella saludable influencia, mientras que las llu-
vias hacen impracticables los caminos que se
cubren de cieno y deshacen las carreteras.
La Manchuria es un inmenso plano cortado
en su parte N. E. por dos cadenas de montañas:
el grande y pequeño Kin-Ngan, que encierran
entre sus brazos la planicie de Tsitsikhar, ca-
pital de Hei-lung-kiang, regada por es célebre
río Nonni, afluente del Soungari que lleva sus
aguas al Amour arriba de Khabaroysk. Un se-
gundo macizo montañoso al este, los montes
Chang-pai, parte de la Siberia en la dirección
de la Corea.

completarlos con la adopción de lo que en Oc-
cidente — y en América — habíase producido
de más perfecto y completo en el orden de las
actividades humanas."

estos grandes progresos, el más eminente, el
"opus magnum," fué la UNIDAD NACIONAL.

Período de expansión y consolidación
(1894-1914
Lo que caracteriza los 20 años de este segun-

La obra del Japón en la Manohuria
Vamos a ocuparnos del obrero, luego estudia-
remos su obra. Esta le honra sobre manera. Al
echar una mirada sobre el mapa de la Manchu-

relativamente pequeña, con relación al total de
la provincia. Tiene, sin embargo, dos grandes
ventajas desde el punto de vista económico: sus
playas se extienden sobre los mares chinos y
mundiales, requeridos de todas partes.
En seguida: gracias al ferrocarril sudmanchu-
riano, habido en herencia de los rusos, en 1905,
los detentores de este ferrocarril están admira-
blemente preparados para descongestionar hacia
esta línea férrea los productos de toda la región,
y, por ella, hasta el mar. Al principio se presen-
taban mayores ventajas, por esta línea la única
en la Manchuria del centro y del sur, puesto que
no había otras lineas concurrentes ni podía ha-
berlas — en virtud de los Tratados hábilmente
pergeñados — y, además por ser ella susceptible
de ser completada por líneas de penetración con-

La China Nueva y la Joven China

amás cambia; no tiene nada que cambiar sin

se nos enseñó a nosotros los occidentales, sobre
ros bancos de la escuela, en los lejanos tiempes

primera que recibió en dote de los l ombres su-

desde su origen: esto, por consiguiente, le dis-
pensaba de evolucionar.

Entre un manchú y un tibetano, entre un
mongol y un setchnounanais, existe una difcren-

tugués. Es imposible confundir, al primer golpe

cambian en cada frontera interior. Los chinos
no hablan una misma lengua.
La China es la imagen viva de una Europa
medioeval. Su régimen es aún hoy, feudal.

CAPITULO VI
La China y los extranjeros
l-i cheng-i
(Máxima china)
De una desconcertante simplicidad era el
concepto que del Universo tenían los antigüos
chinos. Componíase, según ellos, el mundo de
la China y de los "bárbaros." Fuera de esto... el
vacío.
Siendo la China el cerebro y el ombligo del
mundo (en la literatura china el vientre juega
un papel muy importante) a los bárbaros no ha-
bía por qué tenerles consideración alguna, salvo
cuando estos la vapulearon y subyugaron, lo que
aconteció más de una vez. Y aún este caso lo
explicaban diciendo que algunaj divinidad' ma-
ligna favorecía a los "bárbaros". La China es,
por desgracia, el país de las groseras supersticio-
nes, por excelencia, y la Nueva Ohina no desdice
de sus antepasados.
Con esta sencillísima concepción arraigada
fuertemente en el espíritu de los chinos de anta-
ño y hogaño, se cae de su peso que es lícito en-

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.99)

gañar a los bárbaros, i-jen, ceder ante ellos
cuando la resistencia es imposible, para recon-
quistar con la astucia lo que se perdió con la
fuerza; hacerse pequeños ante ellos para armar-
les una zancadilla; servirse de sus rivalidades y
flaquezas para empujar los unos contra los otros:
tales fueron y continúan siendo las prácticas co-
rrientes, elevadas a la categoria de hermosas vir-
tudes del Código de la Moral chino.
Dejo a un lado cosas aún más ridículas.
Afortunadamente para la China, ahi están los
libros de sus Sabios y son ellos de una sublimi-
dad asombrosa.
En la vida privada, en las relaciones comer-
ciales sobre todo, los chinos poseen un sentido
muy profundo de su interés bien entendido, y fal-
tan muy rara vez a su palabra. No por un senti-
miento espiritual, metafísico, familiar a las ra-
zas indo-europeas que odian la mentira — no ca-
recen, en verdad, de otros defectos — por ser
mentira, sino porque las faltas de este género son
más nocivas que el portarse correctamente. La
probidad ordinaria, usual, no es, por ventura, si-
no una probidad de comerciante avisado.
Trasladad, ahora, este procedimiento y otros
muchos parecidos al plan de relaciones de Chi-
na con las potencias extranjeras, y comprende-
réis "a priori" y en conjunto lo que estas rela-
ciones significan. Tomemos, al azar, algún ejem-
plo, entre mil, para comprender mejor esto.
Todo el mundo sabe que, en virtud de los pre-
tendidos "Tratados desiguales," cuya anulación

CAPITULOVII
El verdadero y el falso conflicto chino japonés
Por caminos algo largos. pero ne fuera de
orientación, hemos llegado al meollo del asunto
que motiva este libro, asunto que, por la extrema
resonancia que ha tenido en el mundo última-
mente, y por lo que tiene de obscuro y descono-
cido para el gran público, nos ha parecido do-
tarlo de una previa y rigurosa ubicación.
Con lo hasta aqui expuesto osamos persuadir-
nos de que hemos conseguido nuestro objeto.
Si me expreso de este mdoo al parecer extra-
ño, es porque el título de "Conflicto chino-japo-
nés." no me parece el más exacto en las más au-
torizadas opiniones. Es este un juicio de tal ma-
nera sumario y cómodo, en que las formas lapi-
darias excusan la exploración del fondo de las
cosas, aunque las dejen un tanto inexplicables.
Escribo este libro, y más particularmente este ca-
pítulo, no para los jurisperitos y especialistas en
derecho Internacional; tampoco para los diplo-
máticos, escribo para el grueso público que lee
con el fin de tener una idea, como él

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
114
EL DRAMA DEL EXTREMO ORIENTE
dice, sobre una cuestión que aseguran es sobra-
do grave, pero que juzgan fuera de su alcance, y
casi un jeroglífico.
Quiero, pues, esforzarme en ser metódico pa-
ra ser claro. Hay que proceder en este asunto co-
mo los cirujanos que viviseccionan el cuerpo
humano para ver el verdadero orígen del mal.
Y esto, aparte de diversos motives. para in-
vestigar los orígenes, para averiguar el proceso,
y para procurar por una medicación apropia-
da, curarlo, si aún es tiempo de hacerio: tal
es le caso y es posible esperar bùen resultado.
El "Conficto chino-japonés" parece simple a
primera vista. Pero no es así. Comprende tres
elementos que deben ser examinados y consi-
derados separadamente, como conflictos distin-
tos, o como ramales desprendidos del tronco
principal. Importa no confundirlos ni en el fon-
do ni en la forma, menos aún amalgamarlos,
anuque tengan cierto sentido de analogia, de
igualdad en su decurso. Resultaría aquello (to-
davía no se ha conseguido) que en el lenguaje
vulgar se llama una caja de sorpresas, que se-
ría un error demasiado grave. Parece que esta
confusión se ha aclarado un tanto en las últimas
horas; pero esto no nos permite esperar que
termine el proceso en mucho tiempo si, en Gine-
bra no se cambia de modo de obrar: allí en la
Sesión del Consejo se ha mostrado una diligen-
cia extrema para asegurar el artículo XI de los
Convenios que de ningún modo estaba en peli-
gro. Deberían haberlo colocado en segundo tér-

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
QUE QUIERE EL JAPON! 1QUE QUIERE LA CHINA! 115
mino, orden que le corresponde cronológica y
juridicamente.
Lo primero y más importante, lo que expli-
ca los otros dos, sin el cual tampoco ellos exis-
tirían, es a nuestro parecer, lo siguiente: Este
conflicto proviene a la vez de las relaciones chi-
no japonesas, ante todo las establecidas a partir
del "Tratado de Shimonoseki" (Véase el Anexo)
hasta nuestros dias. El Japón y la China han
hecho, a su turno, el oficio de demandanics y de
demandados. Dejando a un lado la enumeración
de reclamaciones recíprocas (descritas breve-

una medida de autoprotección. Acto precaucio-

CAPITULO VIII
Las improbables complicaciones
No bien la nueva del conflicto chino japonés
se extendió por el mundo — antes de conocerse
exactamente la posición tomada por los dos paí-
ses, en cuanto se tuvo conocimiento del paso en
falso dado por la S. D. N. ; cuando el Japón re-
husó aceptar la manera de ver de Ginebra,
，一
surgieron en todas partes augures para informar
al Universo :o bien, que el Japón cedería; o, si
se declaraba la guerra, ésta no dejaría estar ma-
no sobre mano a Estados Unidos de América ni
a la U. R. S. S., y, probablemente a ninguna
de las dos.
Nunca tuvo mejor aplicación el viejo adagio:
"de lueñes tierras luengas mentiras." Es tan
frecuente encontrar gentes empeñadas en espar-
cir noticias lejanas que no es posible controlar.
En fuerza de estos augurios se puso el Asia en
una decidida conflagración; al Japón aislado
contra la China, flanqueada por Estados Unidos.
socorrida por el Soviet; luego, por hilos miste-
riosos e invisibles, se tenía como cosa cierta que
la Europa tomaba parte en la lucha en defensa

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
¿QUE QUIERE EL JAPON? ¿QUE QUIERE LA CHINA? 139
de los suyos arrojados en medio de aquella pira.
Los primeros días del conflicto fueron de páni-
co. La razón de este terror — por fortuna de
corta duración — fue originada, sin duda. por
un conocimiento rudimentario de la situación
general de Extremo-Oriente, y de los postuiados
que son inherentes al espíritu del vulgo francés,
y aun a lo que no se llama vulgo.
Versan ante todo sobre Rusia y sus relacio-
nes con el Extremo Oriente. Es una idea muy
generalizada el creer inevitable la guerra entre el
Japón y Estados Unidos, por la supremacia del
Pacifico. El Japón es un pais caballeresco: no
olvida fácilmente las injurias. Por otra parte,
los Estados Unidos abusan con frecuencia de su
dohle potencialidad: la financiera y la naval.
¿Enumeraremos? Anexión de las islas Hawai;
ocupación de Filipinas; presión sobre el Japón
en el Tratado de Portsmouth; violencia sin nom-
bre contra los japoneses inmigrados en Califor-
nia, y, finalmente, exclusión total de inmigra-
ción amarilla en Estados Unidos, por la “Immi-
gration Act. Tratado de Wáshington (Cap. 1 y
otros etc. Cuando la copa rebalse y cualquier
pretexto la haga desbordar, que será probable-
mente un incidente nacido del asunto chino; en-
tonces estallará la guerra. Tal es la creencia ca-
si general, que no da lugar la raciocinio, pues
parecen tan fuertes los argumentos aducidos en
su favor.
Sobré los resultados de semejante guerra la
opinión es varia. Para unos la ubicación de las

Si la China pudlera.
Para las personas inteligentes no ha sido
por razón de un súbito amor a la paz, ni por una
suave afección hacia el Pacto de París contra la
Guerra, sino por la certidumbre de que ei ejército

el principio de la sabiduría.

LA MARINA. — La marina de la China se
halla en un estado tan lamentable que casi sería
mejor no mencionarla. Consta de cinco cruceros
de menos de tres mil toneiadas, que datan de
1898; cuatro viejos conducteres de flotillas, tres
o cuatro buques escuela y un trasporte para avio-
nes. Faltos de dinero, rara vez son enviados estos
barcos a limpiar fondos, los cascos se hallan en pé
simo estado así como las máquinas y el aparejo.
Un solo destróyer japonés haría destrozos en esta
vieja ferretería. La razón de tal estado de cosas

CONCLUSION
Comenzóse este libro bajo un signo sinies-
íro de conflicto, que amenazó estallar en una
guerra entre China y Japón y se ternmina en una
atmósfera más serena. Se ha puesto un compás
de espera, preludio de un arreglo posible y tal
vez próximo.
Si he logrado exponer claramente el proble-
ma de Extremo-Oriente, si he puesto de manifies-
to la situación objetiva recíproca del Japón y
de la China, la respuesta a la pregunta colocada
en la carátula del libro. viene sola. Ni una ni
otra de esas preguntas tienen lugar por vez pri-
mera, a propósito de los acontecimientos del 18
de setiembre y de los que le siguieron.
Planteáronse hace cerca de 30 años. El dia
en que el Japón tomó en Manchuria el puesto de
la Rusia vencida, dijo él lo QUE QUERlA. Lue-
go las divergencias de su política respectiva se
han aumentado. A medida que se multiplican las

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
¿QUE QUIERE EL JAPON! ; QUE QUIERE LA CHINA! 159
razones invocadas por cada una de las parles, ei
conflicto parece inevitable. Y tendrá lugar. Lo
que quiere el Japón lo quiere más imperiosamen-
te hoy que nunca., eseribía yo en la “Europe Nou-
velle" de 4 de febrero de 1928. Aunque esta apre-
ciable revista no se haga responsable de las opi-
niones de sus colaboradores, me complazco que
las siguientes lineas hayan aparecido en una dia-
rio que lucha infatigablemente, con criterio se-
lectivo, por el desarrollo de las relaciones inter-
nacionales de la naturaleza de las tesis propues-
tas en Ginebra. Tales tesis y conceptos son, po-
co más o menos, los míos, y creo que sean del
Japón.
"¿ Cuál es — escribia yo -, el objeto que per-
sigue Japón en ia Manchuria? ¿Cuáles sus intere-
ses especiales? ¿ Esos que él jamás permitirá to-
car?"
"Estos que llama "intereses especiales" en
Manohuria no son bases militares para sus vas-
tas conquistas territoriales: son puramente ven-

ANEXOS
Me parece indispensable reunir aqui los pa-
sajes esenciales de los Tratados, Acuerdos y Pro-
tocolos oficiales que tocan de cerca o de lejos, las
cuestiones estudiadas en este libro. La lectura a-
tenta e imparcial de estos documentos podría, en
rigor, dispensar de la lectura del libro. Deberia
bastar, en todo caso, para demostrar cuáles son
las obligaciones y los derechos respectivos de las
parte litigantes. Por via de deducción, en el
confronto de los hechos anteriores y actuales. in-
criminados o pasados sin protesta por las partes
interesadas, es moralmente imposible no ver
quién tiene la razón y quién no la tiene, en el
conflicto chino-japonés.
Los capítulos que priceden servirán de
comentario a los documentos oficiales. pues el
estudio jurídico demandaria volúmenes que no s
hallan a la mano de todo el mundo.
Para mayor comodidad, en lugar de ciasi-
ficarlos por categoría de asuntos a que se refie-
ren, los pondremos pura y simplemente en orden
cronológico.

constituiría un perpetuo obstáculo para una du-
rable pacificación en Extremo-Oriente.
En conscuencia, el gobierno de S. M. el Em-

S. M. el Emperador del Japón, aconsejándole re-
nuncie a la posesión definitiva de la península

IIl. Deolaraolón del Goblerno Japonés
Hecha en Tokio, el 19 de julio de 1895
El gobierno imperial estimando que será
ventajoso para las potencias interesadas en re-
solver cuanto antes las cuestiones pendientes, re-
Jativas a la peninsula de Feng-Tien, y tomando

ellas sabran apreciar debidamente, hace las decla-
raciones siguientes, antes de abrir negociaciones
que el gobierno imperial se propone empeñar sin
demora, directamente con la China:
10. —En el momento de tomar una decisión
relativa al monto de indemnización que debe pe-
dir el Japón a la China, como compensación de
su renuncia a la posesión definitiva de la penín-
sola de Feng-Tien, el gobierno Imperial. no deja-
rã de tener en consideración la situación emba-
razosa de la China y las dificultades que puedan
salirle al paso para encontrar una suma igual al
valor del territorio que debe abandonar.
En consecuencia, el gobierno Imperial, te-
niendo en cuenta las condiciones financieras de
la China, ha reducido, con larga generosidad, su
justa demanda, y ha fijado el monto de dicha in-
demnización en 50 millones de kuping-tales.

Consejo sobre toda circunstancia de naturaleza
tal que pueda afectar las relaciones internacio-
nales, y que amenace, por consiguiente, turbar
la paz o el acuerdo entre naciones que se hallan
en paz.

ART XIII.—Los miembros de la Sociedad
convienen en que si se suscitare entre ellos un

no puede arreglarse de un modo satisfactorio

1001:
Lima's despatch No. 2452 of December 20,
1932, encloses a translation of a newspaper
article entitled "Imperial Japan's Maneuvers
in Peru". It reports the interest of the
press in stories of Peru's purchase of arms
from Japan. In inquiring about Japan's
intentions, it states that once Japan is
established in Callao or has rights on the
Galapagos Islands, the P nama Canal will be
seriously threatened and the coast from Chile
to Panama will be at the mercy of the
Japanese fleet.

of economic penetration and colonization, in order to
utilize these facilities subsequently as a basis for
action against the United States, should a war break
out between the United States and Japan.
Respectfully yours,
AAG/ald
Fred Morris Dearing

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
6losure to Embassy's Despatch No. 2452, December 20, 1932.
American Embassy,
Lima, Peru.
TRANSLATION
of
Article taken from EL TELEGRAFO of Guayaquil,
December 16, 1932.
IMPERIAL JAPAN'S MANOEUVERS IN PERU.
Her Intelligent and aggressive policy may bring
them closer to the Panama Canal.
The fortified Port of Callao and the Galapagos
Islands as strategic centers of the
Japanese squadron.
By OLMEDO ALFARO.
Much is written, and many statements are being ma de
nowadays of Japanese intervention in Leticia. Sometimes
these statements are well-founded, sometimes they are the
figment of the reporter's excessive credulity, easily
deceived by changing international politics.
EL LIBERAL of Cali, November 2, 1932, publishes a
statement that Japanese aviators are making flights over
the upper reaches of the Amazon in the guise of Peruvian
aviators. The better Colombian dailies comment, at some
length, upon the purchase by Peru of arms from Japan - a
transaction which can be readily carried out when both
parties are willing, since Japan has such a large stock
that the quantity for the Peruvian army and navy is not
appreciable to Japan.
Colombian papers state that "Cables received late
this evening at Bogota advise that American Bankers who act
as agents for the guano revenues of Peru, as guarantee for
the service on several loans to Peru, have just entered a
protest to the transaction that Sanchez Cerro had contem-
plated carrying out with Japan to exchange guano for war
material to be used against Colombia".
Peruvian deportees comment upon this phase as follows:
"And now Peru will lose its guano wealth, which is to be
turned over to Japan in exchange for some thousands of use-
less rifles which will be purchased at the rate of 18 pesos
(sic) per rifle, although for the purpose of fiscal accounts,
they will figure at 52 soles per rifle. The Civilistas can
readily leave Peru reduced, in these two ventures (sic), to
one-third of its geographical size and economic importance.
The Civilistas, intent on personal aggrandizement, do not
think of the public welfare. Peru is in the hands of a
dozen families which live in luxury".
(Revista Peru, No. 15, Guayaquil, Calle Bolivar 117.)
Japan

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Japan has important interests in Peru. A Japanese
steamship line running directly between Japan and Peruvian
ports has contributed powerfully to this end.
What can be Japan's intentions in its interference in
Indo-American confliction in the Pacific?
1) The Imperial Japanese fleet constructed
originally for defensive purposes, has no naval bases
outside of the Orient. In case of a military con-
flict between Colombia and Peru-Ecuador, the fortified
port of Callao and the Galapagos Islands will play
a part.
2) The present enemy of Japan is the United
States and the vast Pacific Ocean is the future field
of battle. Once Japan is established in Callao, or
with created rights on the Galapagos Islands, the
Panama Canal will be seriously threatened and the
Western Coast of the American Continent, from Chile
to Panama, would be at the mercy of the Japanese fleet.
All of the Ibero-American countries should make
provisions in view of this future battle-field and of
these two enemy fleets.
Experts in the matter have fixed Brazil as a
propitious field for oriental expansion upon the
Amazon River and valley. The yellow races would come
in to dispute the domination of the Portuguese, who
have been stru gling for centuries towards evolution
and progress.
Argentina, Chile and Mexico: It is problematical
whether these countries would benefit from the Japanese
pagan civilization in the future of our continent; and
finally, the most interested of all of the Ibero-Ameri-
can Republics in the problem - Peru! How do the
Peruvians contemplate Japanese intervention in the con-
flict with Colombia? How do the Peruvians today look
upon the recent intervention of the United States in its
disputes with Chile?
Trans/AAG.
ald

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)

Care Consulado de los E. U. 
SECRET
Lima, Peru,
March 25, 1934.
Major Charles R. Finley,
Quarry Heights, G-2,
Canal Zone.
Dear Major:
Perhaps you will remember the occasion when I visited your
office to request a pass and the final suggestion you made to me in
connection with my visit to Peru.
I have had a most interesting trip so far, much of the time
in Lima, but several jaunts up into the central part of the Sierra to
several quite remote points.
I cannot say that I have encountered any of the anti-American
feeling of which you spoke, although there is a general opinion abroad
that the United States will favor Colombia more than Peru in case of an
armed conflict.
Undoubtedly the Japanese are quite favored by the government,
though perhaps not quite so much so as during the government of Sanchez
Cerro. A certain wing of the government is very decidedly bent on
fomenting close relations with Japan to secure assistance in case of
war, and are thus granting many privileges.
The emigration laws which restrict Japanese have not been
repealed, but there is a gentleman's agreement apparently not to en-
force them, and Japanese keep coming in in large numbers.
Practically all the Japanese business in the country --
restaurants, stores, barber shops, and what not -- are organized as
a sort of cooperative in which apparently the Japanese commercial
attache has a definite part; they are gradually gaining a pre-
21
dominate position in the retail and even wholesale business of the
country. Trade commissions come through frequently, and a permanent
industrial exhibit of Japanese products is maintained. Thus they
have a powerful economic position.
▲ daily paper JIJO is published and a Japanese school
maintained.
Considerable propaganda is put out. During the Manchuko
trouble, beautifully printed booklets in Spanish were profusely dis-
tributed free. The propaganda indicates that the Incas came from the
Orient and that the people are of Oriental extraction, kin to the
Japanese.
-1-

=== Page 253 === (confidence 1.00)
The Japanese do not have to pay an immigration quota as do
the citizens of every other foreign country.
In exchange for guano about 80 million goles of military
equipment were secured recently from Japan. So much guano was sent
out that it seriously affected domestic agriculture, and local growers
are complaining that the government monopoly is not supplying them
sufficient quantities and that that which is supplied is too fresh
to be of any use. Much of the equipment of the Guardia Civil came
from Japan.
It is rumored that the government is negotiating for the
construction of several battleships in Japan.
The best port in Peru and the only good port, perhaps up to
that point south of the canal, is Chimbote. Backed by the Japanese
3
cooperative, the Japanese have, slowly so as not to attract too much
attention, been settling around this bay, until it has a very dominant
Japanese colony.
The Japanese fear the Apra movement, led by Haya de la Torre,
which is very powerful and very nationalistic and which has declared
that it will take means to check the special privileges of the Japanese.
It is rumored that one of the Civilista groups, more or less backing
the present government, has celebrated a pact with the Japanese that
in case of trouble from the Apristas, twenty thousand Japs, approximately
the number in and around Lima, the capital, are pledged to take arms.
I have found no definite corroboration of the report that
Japanese officers are training Peruvian soldiers; though in a case of
international conflict, this might very readily occur.
These details are meager -- I have not looked into the
matter especially -- but I hope that they may prove of some service.
Sincerely yours,
(Sgd.) CARLETON BEALS
Certified "True copy"
RDP

Enclosure No. 1, Embassy despatch 3392, May 2, 1934.
TRANSLATION
THE INTERNATIONAL POLICY OF JAPAN
(Statement of the Japanese Legation in Lima)
The international nolicy of the Japanese Empire
is based on the desire to establish ample cooperation
in the nath of peace, cultivating cordial relations
with friendly countries, causing an increase of con-
fidence towards Janan in international circles and
classifying its moral duty throughout the world.
This thought has heen clearly expressed in the Imperial
Order of March twenty-seventh of last year, when the
Government decided to withdra: from the League of Na-
tions. The Government of Jacan on all its suh jects,
obeying that Imnerial eict, are striving intensely in
favor of vorld peace.
The two American continents, comnosed of countries
friendly to Japan, senarated from it by the Pacific
Ocean, vhich is an imvortant and cuiet road, not only
for ourselves but also for America, en joy close rela-
tions with Jacan, n t only of a cltur:1 and political,
but also of an economic nd social nature; maintaining
confirmed ties in history and tradition for about a
century. The policy of Japan tovards American nations
is to increase the existing harmcny, jointly coopera-
ting for the human well being. Summing up: ma'ntenance
of peace, development of commercial activity, cultiva-
tion of intelligence and mutual understanding and esta-
blishment of reciprocity of interests.
Peru was the first country in South America which
opened relations with Japan, sending a special mission
which

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
which crossed the Pacific in 1873, almost immediately
after Japan opened its ports to international traffic
and signing in that same year a treaty of friendship
and navigation.
That act of long ago produces a plea-
sing impression and has been the basis of the cor-
diality between Peru an" Japan which has increased
day by day for the good of both.
Between these two
countries there is no political question of a dan-
gerous character, n the contrary, they are destined
to maintain extremely pacific and cordial relations,
which are hased on the sympathy, intelligence, and
mutual respect vhich germinate from the innermost
part of the snirit.
This fact is proved repeatedly
by history ever since both neoples came into contact
with each other.
At present, it is not possihle for economic co-
oneration to be lacking between two countries which
are bound together by friendly ties:
Th re is a cer-
tain imoulse which lesds them to exchange products,
as is recuired for their natural eyistence and develou-
ment.
According to this opirion, it may be stated
that the intercha se et een Japan and Peru has not
yet attained a satisfactory level, leaving for the
future a very hroad field of
expectation.
Indeed, the
imports from Japan t Peru were estimated in 1o32 to
he only 1.6% of the total imports into Peru; and in
1933, when the greatest development took place, the
percentage did not attain 4%.
Peru's exports to Japan
in 1933 were estimated to be aproximately 1,000,000
gold soles: from which it results that the exportation
of Peruvian products to Japan in lo33 has increased
more

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
more than eleven fold compared with 1932, and more
than 44 times comnared with 1931.
Hence, the imnorts
and exports between Peru and Janan show a continual
increase. But the exportation of Peruvian products
to Japan is destined to increase in a way that can
be foreseen especially cotton, coffee, wool, etc.,
than's to the Jananese need to obtain articles for
the consumption of its
enormous population and to se-
cure raw materials for its industry. Peru will also
benefit and profit greatly by importing various kinds
of Japanese merchandise recuired by its people.
At present, there are few Peruvians residing in
Japan, but Japan has al ays given all facilities to
Peruvian visitors and residents. In Peru, there is
a considerable Jananese colony. Uner the protection
of the generous Peruvian hosnitality, that colony con-
tributes to the rogress of industry, national re-
sources, and social life in Peru. Thore are certain
anti-Japanese grouns in Peru; however, almost all the
unfavorable comments which are made against the Japa-
nese are based on malice and ignorance.
If the atti-
tude which animates the Jananese colony is observed
carefully and disinterestedly, there is no doubt that
its members are unright and industri us, thereby con-
tributing to the development and orcsperity of the no-
ble Peruvian nation.
The policy of Japan in its relations with Peru is
(1) to encourage relations between the two countries;
(2) to utilize the Japanese residents in a way that is
a benefit to Peru; and (3) to perfect the ideals of
peace and friendship, prosperity and good-will between
the two countries.

Sir:
I have the honor to transmit to the Department
as of possible interest a memorandum of confidential:
information, for which I am indebted to our Consul
JUN 7-
General. The information undoubtedly comes from a
FILED
prominent member of the Diplomatic Commission of
1934
the Constituent Assembly, whom I have lately seen
in company with the Japanese Minister and members
of the Japanese Legation. The information is as
follows.
It is reported that Peru is to acquire
five submaries and five destroyers from Japan
under some provision of Peru's Treaty of
Commerce

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Commeree with Japan. Just what this reaty is
the Embassy has not ascertained at the mom nt
but it will take steps to do so.
It is stated that the plan is for Japan to
give the Peruvian Goveriment a credit of
100,000,000 Soles, against which the five sub-
marines and the five destroyers will be charged.
For this credit Japan is to receive for a period
of ten years rights to the iron deposits in
Southern Peru and a concession to put up at some
convenient point a factory for gun production
and the manufacture of munitions.
The remainder of the report sounds sonewhat
wild but is given to the Department "or what it
may be worth. It is probably an echo of the
stories that have been current on the West Coast
of South America for years. It is that whenever
the general situation so requires, cuador and
Peru will join forces, and the Galapagos Islands
will for considerations be given to Japan as a
naval base.
The story that Japanese aviators are in Peru
is also repeated, and it is stated that Japanese
ammunition is now stored in quantity in the
Department of Junin, that is the highest central
section of Peru, just back of Lima, and that a
number of machine guns have been acquired twice
as powerful as any others now in the possession
of Peru

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
of Peru and capaole of firing l200 shots each.
The Embassy would be inclined to disparage this
information but for the fact that the Japanese are un-
doubtedly seeking along the west Coast of South America
all the iron, cotton, copper, petroleum, and other
natural resources and raw materials upon which it can
establish a claim. It has been evident that it was
willing to do business both with Peru and Colombia
for the purchase of munitions and I am assured by
agents for American aviation co panies and by others
that a considerable number of Japanese agents are
now here actively dealing with various members of the
Government and it is said, I do not know with how
much truth, that graft and bribery are increasing
at a rapid rate. Under all this smoke there would
seem to be some fire, and in view of the latest
announcement of J panese policies and the general
complexion of affairs in the Far East, the reports
the Embassy has received would seem to be at least
worth noting.
Respectfully yours,
Fred Morris Dearing.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AMAY 31 1934
Washington, D.C.
Sir:
I have the honor to remind the Department of
JUN 7-1934
various recent reports from the Embassy conveying in-
FTLED
formation as to the activity of the Japanese in Peru,
and to inform the Department that important Japanese
interests are actively engaged in negotiations looking
to Japanese control of the Peruvian Steamship Company
Compañia Peruana de Vapores.
The head of the Peruvian Company has stated to
Grace & Company that he has had a number of interviews
with the Japanese Minister, that it is impossible for
the Peruvian Steamship Company to continue in business
without

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
without a material subsidy, that the boats are difficult
and expensive to manage, and are in such bad repair that
they are uneconomic to operate.
The head of the Company
is extremely adverse to the rapid and aggressive expan-
sion of Japanese commercial interests that is taking
place in Peru, ano states that rather than carry out
the present project, he will resign his position.
The Government's motive in sanctioning the negotia-
tions with the apanese are primarily the desire to se-
cure a drydock. It is not yet known weether the Japanese
ure to anufacture the drydock and to send it to Peru, or
are to supply the funds for the purchase or construction
of a drydock elsewhere. The Department will recall the
great desire of the reruvian Government for a drydock, the
loss by sinking of the one formerly possessed, and the
disillusionment and disappointment experienced in Peru at
the recent refusal of drydock facilities for the "Bolognesi"
at Panama and the same facilities for the "Grau" something
over a year ago at Panama. At present a public subscription
is being actively cerried on to secure funds for a drydock.
The Government is negotiating with British, Japanese,
Portuguese, and possibly other interests for additional
vessels, and unless a drydock can be obtained, the Peruvian
Navy and the Peruvian Merchant Marine will both be seriously
hampered.
The President of the Peruvian Steamship Company has
approached Grace & Company to inquire whether it would be
willing to come to the Government's assistance in any way
in this matter, saying that he and the directors of his
Company

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Company and, resumably, the Government too would much
prefer to accomplish their purposes through Grace &
Company than by taking tne opportunity held out by the
 panese. The Embassy understands that the Japanese
not only desire the immediate bu iness of the sale or
finaneine of the drydock but ill find the Poruvian
vessels of the greatest ain in vistributing Japanese
merenandise and in colleutin freirnt and, of course,
eventually for political purposes and the ends of
policy. The t snsfer would unuoubtedly sreatly strengthen
the position of the J panese from one end of this country
to the other, and coupled with the very broad provisions
of the Treaty of 1895 woul: enable Japan to bring about
a tremendous expansion ot ner commercial position in Peru
and probably do a great deal to break down all the pro-
hibitory provisions a ainst the immigration of Japanese.
The colony in the country already amounts to something
over 30,000, and it may be considered that the Japanese
have a more firm foothold in this country and thus on the
West Coast of South America than at any other point.
Grace & Company explained to me that the Japanese
Steamship Lines, for which incidentally the Grace Company
are agents, could use the Peruvian vessels in Peruvian
coastwise trade with the very greatest advantage. These
vessels, once they are fixed up and restored to good
condition in the very drydock in question, could be so
operated that the large steamers coming out from Japan
need only touch at Callao where they could leave their
freight

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
freight to be distributed by te smaller vessels and pick
up all the freight that had been gathered for export.
Having this business has a steady backlog, the vessels
could then under Peruvian registry carry on a competition
which would put practically all other vessels out of
business.
The Embassy understands that Grace & Company are
bringing this matter to the attention of the New York
Office, ane suggesting thet the Company get in touch with
the State Department, the Commerce Department, and the
Navy Department. It is believed that it is hardly neces-
sary to point out again the si nificance of the negotie-
tions in question for Japan in case there should be any
possibility of a war for that country in any quarter.
As a reservoir of raw materials and a market for a
steadily increasing commerce South America, and particu-
larly the est Coast, is a re ion of the greatest sig-
nificance for Japan.
respectully yours,
Fred Morris Dearing.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

DIVISION ORLATIN-AMERICAN AFFAIRS
SPECIAL
October 26, 1934.
MR. DUNN
The attached despatch No. 3634 of
October 10, 1934, from Lima, sets forth
the present status of Japanese penetra-
tion in Peru. It is unofficially esti-
mated that there are in the neighborhood
of 50,000 Japanese in Peru at the present
time.' Until last week Japan's commercial
treaty with Peru, concluded in 1924, per-
mitted unrestricted immigration. We have
just been tnformed telegraphically that
Peru has denounced that treaty. The
Embassy reports that Japanese infiltra-
tion has been rapid and that it has in-
vaded every form of economic life in
Peru. The Japanese Legation at Lima,
including six diplomatic officers, is
larger than any other mission in Peru.
A Japanese newspaper is published in Peru
and a large Japanese grammar school has
been established in the capital. Japanese
societies have important agricultural
holdings in the Chanchamayo Valley. A
cotton plantation at Huaral owned by a
Mr. Okada employs 2,000 laborers, almost
all of whom are Japanese. 25 out of 95
department stores in Lima are in Japanese
hands; 17 out of 41 hotels are operated
by Japanese. Over 400 small fruit stores
are owned by Japanese and out of 260 cafes
and
NOV 14 1934

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF LATIN-AMERICAN AFFAIRS
MEMORANDUM
一2一
and bars, 209 are run by Japanese.
Chimbote, the harbor of which is said
to be the best on the Pacific coast
between Panama and Pedro Monte, Chile,
has a population of between two and
three thousand persons, of which about
25 per cent are Japanese,who own over
one-half of the small establishments in
the city.
Opposition to Japanese in the face
of the above situation is growing in Peru.
The fear is expressed that unless a halt
is called the Japanese may ultimately
displace lawyers, doctors, and even priests,
and public authorities. The Peruvian
populace in times of civil strife is
prone to mob Japanese and Chinese and to
loot their shops. The Peruvian press
has lately taken up a crusade against
the Japanese. The Government's recent
decision to enforce the laws passed in
1932 and 1933 requiring 80 per cent of
the personnel of all commercial and indus-
trial establishments to be Peruvian is
said to be aimed at the Japanese. Agri-
cultural interests are insistent that
the terms of the law be extended to
agricultural enterprises.
From

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.95)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF LATIN-AMERICAN AFFAIRS
MEMORANDUM
-3-
From the trade point of view, Japanese
activity in Peru, which was visited in 1932
by a Trade Mission, has been very success-
ful. Figures for i933 show importation to
the value of 5,037,260 soles from Japan
compared with a value of 1,178,886 soles
during 1932, a gain of 327 per cent. The
percentage of the total value of Peruvian
import trade supplied by Japan increased
from 1.2 per cent in 1929 to 4.9 per cent
in 1933, and to 5.5 per cent during the
first five months of 1934. Japanese imports
into Peru are of the familiar type, mostly
the cheaper grades of cotton textiles, and
such miscellaneous consumer's goods as
electric light bulbs, rubber footwear,
hardware, et cetera.
S. E. Grummon.

Kinship between Incas and Japanese.
To develop good feeling and sympathy in Peru, the
Japanese have made great use of the legend which is
perhaps without factual basis thet they and the Inces
are of the same enthnographic origin, and they point
with

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
with pride to the sun emblem common to the two countries.
They have propagated extensively this legend of kinship
between the Incas and Japanese, developing in every
possible way the sentimentality and the mutual ad-
miration which naturally spring from the feeling of
blood relationship. In this connection, it is of
interest that when the foreign colonies in Lima made
gifts to Peru in 192l, on the occasion of the centenary
of its independence, the J panese colony donated a
statue of the great Inca, Manco Capac, founder of
the Ince Empire, with the evident intention of devel-
oping this legend.
This theory of common origin was further developed
by rrancisco A. Loayza, a Peruvian author, in his book
entitled "Manko kapa", published in 1926 when Japanese
influence was in its ascendance in Peru.
 The line of
reasoning developed by this author is that ..anko kape,
founder of the Peruvian nationality was a foreigner;
that he and his people spoke a strange foreign language
which he takes pains to establish philologically as
Japanese. The author describes the pre-Colombian
voyages across the North Atlantic, and explains the
comparative ease of Trans-Pacific crossings aided
by the Japanese current. According to the legend,
the landing of Manko Kapa was at Arica whence he led
his followers to Lake Titicaca, the cradle of the
Inca race.

the Chavin

the Chavin culture, as its homage in connection with
the IV Centennial of the founding of the Jity of Lima

The Peruvian "Centro cultural

1934 adopted a resolution of thanks for this signifi-
cant donation, and designatcd a committee including
high ecclesiastical dignitaries and men of letters
to cooperate in all official acts relating to the
inauguration of this monument.
Treaty of Amity and commerce.
Another important link between Peru and Japan, and
one which has enormously facilitated the relations be-
tween the two countries, is the reaty of Amity, Commerce
and Navigation, negotieted at Lima on eptember 30, 1924,
and ratified by Peru on January 8, 1929. The life of
this reaty was fixed for five years from the exchange
of ratifications, after which it would continue in force
indefinitely subject to termination by either party any
time after twelve months' advance notice.
This Treaty seems to favor Japan on every point
and, in view of the growing feeling against the Japanese,
it would not be at all surprising if notice of cancella-
tion by Peru were given sooner or later. rhe present
situation points in this direction.
Japanese Immigration into Peru.
The paramount interest of J&pan, which naturally
must color its policies, is directed to the solution of
the problems

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
the problems created by a population that has increased

. As all evaileble land in
Japan is already occupied, outlets had to be found to
take care of the excess population.
When the Japenese found themselves barred by re-
strictions and inmi retion laws in the United States,
Canada, and Australia, they began to look for new
fields aside from Korea and China to take care of their
surplus population which was increasin at the rate of
700,000 or 300,000 each year. Japanese attention was
directed to South Americe as a possible outlet for her
surplus population. Srazil and Peru were considered
because of the mild climate in the desirable sections
of these republics coupled with liberal laws which
would not interfere in any way with their activities.
In the early years Japanese inmigration to Peru
was sporadic. In 1897 ebou" 1200 Jepanese 1aborers
were admitted into the country for employment in the
coastal valley estates. Several hundred were intro-
duced a few years later for employment in the rubber
plantations of the Orient. The steady infiltration
of Jepanese, both regular and clandestine, continued.
Estimates place the number of Japanese in Peru in 1920
in the neighborhood of 5,0o0. For the next few years
or so a stream of from 500 to 1,000 Japanese immigrants
per year flowed into the country. In the period from
1925 to l931 Japanese immigration into Peru reached its
maximum. Since then there has been a decrease in the
movement

movement, said to be due to a diversion of immigration

promise.

Japanese Aid to Emigration.
For the past ten years, Japan has paid great
attention to Peru as a field for immigration and ex-
pansion. It is understood that immigrants to Peru are
assisted by cooperative organizations or societies
either sponsored by the Japanese Government or connected
with the emigration service in Japan, which work in
conjunction with the Japanese missions and consulates
abroad. According to the Peruvian immigration laws
all alien inmigrants who have S/ 2,000 in their
possession

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
possession are allowed to enter. It is generally under-
stood that this amount is advanced by the Japanese co-
operative society which makes all the arrangements for
the immigration, transportation, assignment to positions
in commerce or agriculture and, when necessary, exten-
sion of financial assistance to the immigrants. The
immigrants are received at the steamers, they are taken
charge of, and they are aided to set themselves up and
get a start in their new surroundings.
A large Ja panese grammar school has been established
in Lima, to which the children can be conveniently sent
for their education.
Peruvian students can matriculate
but the teachers are for the most part Japanese.
There are two Japanese newspapers in Lima, IPPO
and PERUJIHO, which are saia to have an extensive cir-
culation in Japanese circles.
Growing Opposition to Japanese.
Notwithstanding that the Peruvian Government has
openly favored the Japanese Government, amongst the
public there has always been a certain hatred of
orientals. Whenever a revolution or riot occurred,
the populace was prone to mob and loot Japanese and
Chinese grocery and food shops. On November 7, 1931,
the Japanese Legation was even attacked and stoned by
a mob said to have been communists. This was at the
time of the Chinese trouble.
The Japanese officials realize that possible
agitation against their people may result in a closing
of the

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
of the doors to their countrymen and for this reason,
immigration las been kept at a steady flow but not
so intensively encouraged as to attract attention
and cause criticism.
It is very evident that the movement against
the Japanese in Peru is gaining ground. Most of the
press of Lima and callao, exce t L CoHERCIO which is
the leading newspaper, has taken up a crusade against
the Japanese. EL CCMERCIO is understood to avoid
publishing any articles against Japan on the ground
that it is inconvenient as a matter of public policy.
This probably accounts for the extensive advertising
of Japanese firms in that paper and its issue of e
special number on the Japanese national holiday. There
is a growing feelin: that the entry of Japanese labor-
ers into the country should be controlled in the same
manner that the Chinese are restricted by the Porras-
Wu Ting Fang protocol of August 28, 1909. With this
end in view, a society against the Japanese supported
by agrarian circles has been formed known as the Anti-
Japanese League. It has its own paper, LA TIERRA,
which favors exclusion and restriction against any
further acquisition of agricultural land. However,
before much progress can be made along these lines,
it will be necessary to abrogate the Peruvian-Japanese
Treaty which insures such liberal treatment for the
Japanese. It is barely possible that there may be
early developments in this connection.

Agriculture.
The Japanese in Peru have been especially active in
agriculture. They have made important strides in acquir-
ing some of the best lands on the coast. In several
valleys - Chillon and Chancay - t ey already dominate,
and in others - Janete and Huacho - they have made great
progress in establishin: themselves as farmers. It is
generally known that they pay more for land, elso higher
rentals, than the Peruvians. The general impression
has gotten about that they acquire farms at prices
actually higher than they are worth. This is said to
be made possible by the overnment and cooperative
backing which they receive in getting permanently
established. The Chinese Minister, who should be well
informed on a matter of this kind, corroborated to me
that the Japanese Consulate General directed the Japa-
nese cooperative society which makes loans and arrange-
ments for the Japanese to immigrate and take up farming.
The Ja anese Societies of Lima and Callao purchased
over two years ago from The Peruvian Corporation exten-
sive land areas for agricultural pu poses in the Chancha-
mayo Valley, near the Perené Colony. Japanese families
from Callao and others arriving from Japan are being
sent constantly to settle on this land. On one or two
occasions, members of Congress have denounced this policy
of Japanese "economic penetration of the Orient of Peru",
but no official action has been taken on this score.

naval base. This probably arises from the fact that
it is the best harbor on the Pacific Coast between
Panama and Pedro Monte, Chile, as it is ample in size,
easy of access, well sheltered and there is plenty of
depth.
The population of Chimbote and neighborhood does
not exceed 2000 or 3000 persons of which about 25% are
Japanese who own about 50 or 60% of the small estab-
lishments, including food stores, cafés, hotels, barber
shops

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.95)
-23-
shops, etc.. There are also some Japenese laborers on
the adjoinine farme tut t ey heve not taken possession
as yet of the farms in this vicinity to the same ex-
tent as they have in some of the valleys more to the
South.
It is significant thet on August , 1934 Deputy
Ramos in a speech in the Constituent Assembly made
the statelent thet "ue to its geogrephical situation
Chimbote has aroused the interest of foreign "overn-
ments". Thereupon, he presented a cill that a dry
dock should be construeted there and that Chimbote
should be converted into a Peravian military port.
Conclusion.
To supplement the foregoing survey of some of
the ph ses of Peruvian-Ja anese relations, I enclose
2/
copies of reports prepared at my request by Jommerciel
Attaché Julien D. Smith and by Vice Consul Arthur D.
3/
Jukes. Mr. Smith's report covers the commercial situa-
tion vhereas Mr. Jukes' report describes at length
the port of Chimbote and the shipping situation as it
refers to the Japanese, and it touches upon their
participation in the commerce and agriculture of Peru.

JAPANES. TRADE ACTIVITIES
IN P.RU
Imports of Jepanese products into Peru have shown a
very substantial increase since the  iddle of 1933; final
figures for last year (not including parcel post) amounted
to S/5,037,260 compared with S/1,178,886 in 1932, a gain
of 327%. While they still constitute a small percentage
of total Peruvian importations, the trend is upward and
signifies a threat of the displacement of a large part
of the market for certain European and American products.
The following table shows r'eruvian imports from
Japan for the last five years and the first five months
of 1934. Figures for 1929 through 1932, include pereel
post imports, the others do not.

Note:- Included in the above figures for 1933 are 219 tons

These were special shipments secured in exchange for
