during

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
during the early months of 1934, the upward trend of
Japan's share in the import trade of reru is all the
more significant.
There are three main reasons for the gain in
Japanese imports into Peru:- (1) low prices made possible
by low wages in the country of origin and by the de-
preciation of the yen; (2) an organized trade arive on
the South American markets; and (3) an effective system
of distribution through dealers und retailers of Japanese
nationality。
The factor of low prices is by far the most importent,
Peru has elways been & price market; low initial cost is
given first consideration b. the  uvian buyer, rather
than quality or dura ility. rhe influence of low prices
has been strong enough to offset to a great extent a
certain antipathy in this country toward the apanese
people and things Jepanese.
Japan has mede & systematic drive for trude on the
South American market since the latter part of 1933. More
Japenese businessmen have visited this country. rhere
have been at least, two distinct trade missions - a
cotton spinners' and manufacturers' mission which ar-
rived here in December l933, and a military mission
which came in May of this year (Peru also sent a military
mission to Japen in 1933). The textile commission was
particularly successful in establishing agencies and in
obtaining information of value to Jepenese cotton goods
exporters with the result that Peruvian imports of cotton
goods

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
goods exoorters with the result that Peruvian imports of
cotton goods have inereased enormously even to the ex-
tent of seriously affecting the demana for the domestic
product (Production of cotton goods is the principal
domestic manufacturing industry). he service of Jap-
anese steamship lines to South America hes increased.
It is the seneral belief here that the steamship com-
panies are heavily subsidized anc can pass on part of
this benefit to exporters in the form of low freight
rates.
Note:- CONFIDETIAL - The J panese military mission
above referred to gave some indicetion that they might
be interested in the purch&se of the Peruvian Steamship
Company.
The effective distrioution of Japanese merchandise
in Peru has been increased largely through deelers and
retailers of Japanese nationality, although many agencies
have been placed with commission merchants and importers
of all nationalities. The number of new apanese firms
established in Lime and Callao was 15 in 1932, 20 in
1933, and 11 in the first six months of 1934. 1hese
figures are for compenies only, individual merchants
not being included as they are not obliged to register.
Furthermore the figures do not include all concerns, as
some of the Japanese houses have organized as stock
companies, and, having adopted Spanish names, cannot be
distinguished from concerns of other nationality. Japa-
nese retailing firms have attracted the public to
increased patronage by advertising prices lower than
their

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
their competitors for certain articles of wide consumption
including American made toothpeste, razor blades, silk
hosiery, etc. Once the public become tware of generally
lower prices in the Japanese stores, they can readily be
switched to Japenese products of still lower cost.
The Japenese competition has been felt most in cotton
goods, made-up articles of cotton, end cement, although
small but steady sains are being made in the trade in
incandescent bulbs, miscellaneous electrical goods, storage
batteries, rubber footwear, toothpaste, pharmaceutical
specialties, enamel ware, miscellaneous hardware, clocks
and watches and oil cloth. United States trade has been
substantially affected only in cotton piece goods, and
cotton hosiery. rollowing are statistics showing the
principal items of import from J pan accoraing to the
classification of the Peruvian Import Tariff schedule.

Cotton Yarns.
In spite of strenuous efforts on the part of Jananese manu-
facturers of cotton yarns, no sales are yet reported. Samples
of mercerized and gassed yarns of Japanese origin have been re-
ceived by practically all the local knitting plants, but they
have not yet been induced to buy through cheapness of price,
fearing that interruptions of plants and waste of materials
may cause them heavy losses.
Portland Cement.
Figures given below showing Peruvian imports of Portland
cement reveal an extraordinary gain by the Japanese product.
This has largely been at the expense of Denmark and Germany,
since American cement (except white and special cement) has
been too high-priced for this market for several years.
Imported

=== Page 312 === (confidence 1.00)
-9-
Imported cement cannot compete with the local manufactured
product in the Lima-Callao and Central Peru region but can
undersell the latter in other parts of
the country, due to
high coastwise freight and handling costs.
Recent quotations
for Japanese cement have been as low as $1.50 (U.S. currency)
per barrel C.I.F. Peruvian ports.
The local cement manufactur-
ing company has become alarmed and is asking for greater
tariff protection.

Iron and steel Products
The first evidence of &pen's attempts to participate
in the iron tnc steel trade was the recent receipt by local
machinery houses of direct offers from a anese anufacturers.
No representatives seem to have yet been appointed for the
sale of apanese iron and stell manufactures, but negotia-
tions are known to be under wey et present with Lime agents.
There is a specific case of a well known manufacturer's rep-
resentative having recently left for J: pen to negotiate the
assumption of an exclusive iron and steel products line.
The principal items offered by Jepenese exporters are
the following:
Iron sheets (plain and corrugated)
Iron bers (round, square, angles and plates)
Wire nails (countersunk checkered heads)
Iron wire (principally the smallest sizes of
less than a millimeter in diameter. No. 18
and other small sizes).
Japanese quotations on the above products with the
exception of the last named, are not more favorable than
American. Their pricew on No. 18 wire are reported to
average ten percent under American exporters' quotations.
This

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
This accounts for the fact that, so far, none of the large
machinery houses has been induced to place & trial order,
notwithstanding the exceptionally liberal terms on which
the offers were made. These terms run from payment on
long periods of time to shipment on a consignment basis,
the latter being offered in the majority of cases.
It is learned from trade sources that the first ship
ments of Japanese sheets heve not been favorably received,
as the quality is reported to leave much to be desired.
This fact and higher quotations on Japanese goods are con-
tributing to hinder the activity of Japanese firms in so-
liciting business from Peruvian mercents. As yet there
is little concern expressed in business circles about
Japanese trade in iron and steel products, but if Japan
is later successful in under uoting her competitors and
in improving the quality of her goods, there is e danger
that this competition may become important.
Japan in Peruvian Export Trade.
While Japar has not yet become an important factor in
Peru's export trade, exports of Peruvian products to Japan
have shown substantial gains within the last two years.
Following are the official customhouse figures for the past
five years and the first five months of l934:-

Exports from Peru to Japan showed a large increase in
l933, due principally to direct exportation of raw cotton.
Japanese

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
Japanese spinners have long been familiar with the Peruvian
staple but heve bought small stocks in Liverpool. A local
cotton brokerage firm has recently started this direct
business for sale on arrival. Leading exports from Peru
to Japan in 1933, were 800 metric tons of cotton, 58 tons of
alpaca wool, 41 tons of coffee and l0 tons of cottonseed
cake.
The general impression is that the large increase in
exports to Japan is the result of guano shipments. It is
true that in 1933, some 8,000 tons of guano were shipped
to Japan in exchange for armaments, but the guano e ports
do not appear in the export statistics as the guano busi-
ness is in the hands of a semi-official organization. No
rich guano will be available for export in l934, or for
two or three more years.
Miscellaneous Activities.
Large Japanese interests started buying land in the
important cotton-producing valley of chancay in l926, and
since that time the Japanese influence has spread until
it has become dominant in that particular valley. Attempts
are now being made to purchase or lease land in other
valleys.
A local Japanese firm is one of the principal domestic
manufacturers of straw and felt hats.
ror several months there has been a story that a branch
of the Yokohame specie bank would be started in Lima, but
this is still in the rumor stage.
Local Attitude Toward the Japanese.
There has always been a certain antipathy in Peru toward
the Japanese

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
the Japanese and the Chinese. Whenever a revolution, near-
revolution, or riot occurs, a certain element in the popu-
lation is prone to sack and loot Japanese and chinese
grocery and food stores.
The recent enforcement of the so-called eighty percent
Peruvian employement legislation (laws specifying that every
concern must employ at least eighty percent Peruvian personnel,
and pay eighty percent of the payroll to reruvians) is aimed
primerily at the Japanese and chinese. There is also a
movement on foot to stop the J&panese encroachment in trade
and manufacturing, by stricter immigretion requirements and
by higher tariff protection to local industries. The
domestic cotton mills and the local cement plent are endeavor-
ing to have the Government raise the import duties on similer
articles from abroad. This has been the result of Japanese
competition.
(Signed)
Julian D. Smith
Julian D. Smith

INTRODUCTION.
Referring to the Embassy's request that this Consul-
ate General investigate certain features of Japanese
activities (immi ration and commerce) in Peru, in accord-
ance with the instru tion received by the Embassy from
the Department of State, tle following notes have been
compiled along the lines suggested.
Much of the information nerewith has been confirmed
but such of it as nas not been confirmed, has come in the
majority of cases from what are considered good so urces,
and is included herein.
The information which was receivel in the Department,
apparently paints an alarminc picture of the Japanese sit-
uation in Peru. Hovever, it would seem, as a result of
comparatively thorough inquiry that while the ingression
of Japanese into tnis country crcates dangerous situation,
at least at th: present t ime the danger would appear to
lie in entirely diferent channels than those mentioned
in the comunication which reached the War Department.
The serious aspect at the present time, as we see it, con-
sists principally in a steadily augmenting penetration of
Japanese into agriculture and the petty commerce of Peru,
the absorbing of the country's fine agricultural lands by
these nationals, and the apparent inertia on the part of
the Peruvian Government to apply restrictions which would
limit the Japanese in their increasing activities. The
only recent overt restriction placed against foreigners is
the

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.96)
-2 -
the 80 percent-national requiremnt, but t is appears to
be ineffective to cope with the Japenese infiltration.
CHIMBOTE.
In a communication which reache the War Department
from a private so urce, an informant stre sed the possi-
bility, or the probabilit of the Japanese taking over
the Teruvian port of Climbote as eitn  a naval base or
as a narbor.
 The impression iven in the above-mentioned
21
comnunication wus that the 'i re himbote distric t was
popul tec prineipully by Japanese, and hinted that these
0
were more r les masquerali un ler the occupations of
(1
farmers, laborers und  ine's men. An effort has been
made to confirm this, and inquiries ave been made of con-
tacts who are entirely familiar with the Chimbote district,
and in some cases, with those who have interents there.
The only information which we have thus far been able to
gather, leads us to beliove tnat the information alleging
Chimbote to be a Japanese base is exaggerated.
It is the understanding of this office that most of the
Japanese in the Chimbote distriet are small business people,
owners of barber snops, otels, smell stores, "chinganas",
"pulperfas", bars a d cafés, et cetera. It is stated that
roughly sixty per cent of the small business in the port
is in the hands of the Japanese. This is also understood
to hold true at the port of Samanco, the neighboring town
six miles to the southward of Chimbote. The entire popula-
tion of Chimoote and its immediate vicinity would hardly
exceed two thousand, of which, we are told, between fifteen
and twenty percent are Japanese.
Chimbote

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
Chimbote is the outlet for the Santa Valley, an exten-
sive and fertile region, levoted largely to sugar and cotton.
The port is conneeted by rail with Taolones, 57 kilometers
up the valley, and Huallance, with an additional 8l kilo-
meters of railway line. There is also a 30-kilometer
branch line connecting tie latter town with Chuquicara.
Approximately half of tle Santa Valley is said to be-
lone to the Hacienda "Tambo Real", now in the nands of the
Banco Italiano and the Banco Aleman 'ransatlantico, as a
result of mortgage foreclosures. Somethine over one-fourth
of the valley beloncs to a large hacienda which formerly
belonged to members of either the Legufa or Vinces family,
but which we are told was confiscated and it is our under-
RICLY
standing that it is now operated by local interests. The
remainin section of the valley is owned and cultivated
by a few Peruvian inter sts having relatively lurge hold-
C
ings. From where the Santa Valley extenis i to the "Callejon
de Huayllas", this territory is far ed almost in its entire-
ty by Peruvian landowners. In short, such information as
this office has obtained, indicates that apart from the
petty com erce in the imnediate vicinity of Cnimbote and
Samanco, that the Japanese have not penetrated into the agri-
culture to any alarming extent in the Chimbote district, and
there is no immediate danger of their doing so, according to
present indications.
The same conditions are said to exist in the Nepeña dis-
trict, a neighboring valley, which extends inland from Sam-
anco. Almost the entire valley belongs to the Lockett in-
terests, under the name of Hacienda "San Jacinto", and the
ramaining,

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.96)
- 4 -
remaining, cultivable lands are held by Peruvians, with
few if any Japanese engaged in a riculture. As is the case
with Chimbote, the stores, hotels, vars, barber shops, and
"chinganerfa" in general, in Samanco, are con trolled prin-
cipally by the Japanese. Furthermore, they appear to be the
only ones in that region who can make any money in trading
1
with the poorer Peruvian laboring classes. Mr. Richard Lock-
ett informs us that he formerly had about 2o0 Japanese La-
borers, but that for the past fifteen years they have had
no oriental labor either at "San Jacinto" or at the "Santa
2
Barbara" hacienda. Ine bulk of these lands are devoted to
sugar cultivation.
What has buen stated about Chimbote being an excellent
人一上
port is quite true. It is decidedly the best harbor in Peru,
0
and having seen most of the principal ports between the Canal
Zone and Puerto Montt, Chile, it is probably one of the best
C
on the Wost coast of South America. A description of the
harbor may enable those who have never seen it to obtain some
idea about it and its merits.
The bay is over six miles from north to south, recedes
about three miles, is well closed in, sheltered, with plenty
of water, and is easy of access. The principal entrance to
the harbor is one and three quarters miles in width, sounding
14 to 19 fathoms, i.e., ample water for any ocean-going ves-
sel. The bay itself sounds four and a half to ten fathoms,
with mud and mud-sand bottom, which offers good holding
ground. Barring two five-fathom patches close in to shore,
the bay is free from all shoals and obstructions.
The harbor is closed in on the northward by Mt. Chimbote
(on the mainland- altitude 1,640 feet); Blanca Island, ex-
tending in a northerly-southerly direction one and thr ee-
quarters miles in length and having a high ridge ranging in
altitude

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
altitude from about three hundred fifty to six hundred
sixty feet. To the southward of the passage, the Ferrol
Islands extend in a west-northwesterly direction, and af-
ford ample protection against the southwesterly wind, swell
and current. The southern boundary of the bay is formed by
Mt. Division and a ridge (altitude - seven hundred eighty
to thirteen hundred thirty feet) extending out in a general
113
northwesterly direction. This mountain and ridge are con-
nected with the mainland by a low, sandspit. We have flown
over this harbor and have also had an opportunity to check
this information in the United States Hydrographic Office
Coast Pilot and detail charts for the port, with Captain J.
Reznik, master of the American S.S. CaiDoR, as well as with
other masters.
As stated in the enclosures to the instruction to the
Enbassy, the general physical, topo raphical and strategi-
cal set-up of the port of Chimbote, when viewed from the
angle of military or naval defense, is superb. As a matter
of fact, only a short t ime ago a measure was suggest d in
the Peruvian Congress, providing for the establishment of a
Peruvian Naval base at that port. The motion was brought up
in the session of Congress on Au ust 8, 1934, to recommend
that the port of Chimbote be considered as a base for the
construction of a graving dock, and that a military and
naval base be established in the port.
It is possible that what may have prompted the allega-
tions made regarding Chimbote with respect to the Japanese
taking over Chimbote, was a rumor which was being circulated
in Lima and Callao about eighteen months ago that certain
Japanese

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6 -
Japanese interests (I believe it was Okura & Co., Ltd.,
of Tokyo, but am not certain) were negotiating to lease
the port for a hundred years. According to the rumor, a
small port works was to have been constructed, the lessees
of the port planned to exploit coal fields in the interior.
The rumor, however, died a natural eath, and apparently
nothing hus come of it. It reaclhed t is ofice about a year
or so aco thr suh a. American livin: in the Chimbote dis-
trict, conneeted with minin interests, und it is possibly
from this individl that a reat part f the misinformation
about chimbete has be en derived. As the so uree of the in-
formation was kn wn to this ofti :e as unreliable, the alle-
gations had already been di carded as nst well founded, or
rather, exaggerated.
SHIPPING.
With respert to the exist ing Japanese steamship serviees
C
on this coast, the only regular line operatin a sc eauled
service between Peru and the Crieit is the Nippon Yusen Kaisha
of Tokyo. This lire operates thr e comparatively l rge ves-
sels, one of whieh is at tI at word in up-to-late o r-
utine and cur o n lin tecselo on this cort. The t ree
vessels in  le t ar:. HEIYO MARU (the newest of
this fleet), an the stearers RAKUYO MaRU, and BOKUYO MARU.
Outward ports of call (uc. eduled) are Hongkon, Moji, Kobe,
Yokohama, Honolulu, Hilo, San Francisco, Los An eles, Manzan-
illo, La Libertad, Balboa, Buenaventura, Callao, Pisco, Mol-
lendo, Arica, Iquique and Valparaiso. The homeward bound trip
schedules calls at Iquique, Callao, Balboa, Los Angeles, Part-
land, Yokohama, Moji and Hongkong. These vessels also touch
at Peruvian cotton ports northbound, if sufficient cargo of-
fers.
Vessels

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
Vessels of this Japanese line we understand, are now
discharging from two to four hundred weight tons of in-
bound cargo per voyage, or say, a ro gh double of those
figures, in so far as revenue tonnage is concerned.
For
a time the Nippon Yusen Kaisha vessels ran so light that
they were showing six to eight feet of water line and it
is frankly a mystery as to how the line managed to keep
them running, even with the strong subsidy. As to the
question of Ja anese subsidizati on, we have peen unable
to le arn much accurate information, but recall from an
article published some time ago i the New York Marine
NOO
Journal that Japanese ships rank with the most heavily sub-
sidized shipping in the world.
The mainstay of the Japanese trade on the Peruvian
coast, however, has been, up until the past year or eighteen
months, comprised largely of third class passenger business,
of which there is a fair amount even since the Chinuse boy-
cott of the N. Y. K. Line. This boycott has existed almost
since the beginning of the Manchukuo seizure. While this
is apart from the present report it might be wor th mention
that there is a quarter-cast Englishman here, working in
conjunction with the Dollar Line.
This individual speaks Chinese, is probably part Chinese,
and is in close contact with the colony. It is understood
that he is sapping both the N.Y.K.'s passenger and frei ht
business, which formerly moved on the Japanese vessels, and
has been turned, to a great extent through other channels,
transshipping via the Dollar Line at Panama. This has
meant the loss of lucrative trade to the Nippon Yusen Kaisha.

Peruvian Steamship Company.
It has been ai'ficult to secure much information re-
garding this subject other than that during Murch and
April, and possibly previous to that time there was some
negotiation on the part of Japanese interests to buy
the Peruvian Stea ship Company (Compañia Peruana de Va-
pores of Callao) and with it, the rignt to operate it in
the local coastwise trade, on more or less the sa e basis
as it i. buing operated at the present time. There were
no rumors of this prospective transaction floating about
Callao and about the only source of information this of-
fice had was Mr. George J. Leovy of W. R. Grace & Company
TCULY
who was naturally intensely interested in any such nego-
tiation. In June, Ir. Leovy stated that Pedro Bustamante
Santisteban nad romised him a memorandum, outlining the
details of the exchange of corresponuence on this subject.
Leovy, nowever, apparently did not receive the memorandum,
but the other day he informed us tnat he had spoken with
Bustamante Santisteben, and that the latter was of the
opinion that he ad definitely killed any prospects of sale
of the Peruvian Steaship Company.
Pursuant to this subject, and at about the same time,
two other bits of news reached this office: namely, the
establishment of a new Japanese service of the Nippon Yusen
Kaisha, in the Philippine Islands/Gulf of Mexico trade,
accepting all transshipment cargo possible for the west
coast of South America as well as Gulf cargoes. It is
reasonable enough to believe that had the Japanese purchased
the Compañia Peruana de Vapores, an excellent transshipment
connection might have been established, which would have
seriously

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.98)
- 25-
seriously affected cargo carried in American vessels and
would have affeoted trade as well. About the same time,
one of the Peruvian Naval officers sent a memorandum to
this office relative to the rumored attempts made on the
part of the Japanese Government to buy the Galapagos Islands
from Eouador, in order to establish colonies and a naval
base. Whether there is any relation between these three
points, or not, we do not know, but it hardly seems likely.
The Japanese service between the Philippines and Gulf
of Mexico was covered by this Consulate General's report
of July 25, 1934, entitled: "New Philippine/Gulf Service of
the N.Y.K. Steamship Line", a copy of which was submitted
to the Embassy. Hovever, we are inoluding herewith for the
sake of convenience, a copy of a private circular letter
sent by the Ni: pon Yusen Kaisha at Tokyo to its agents,
relative to the inauguration of this service.
"(Circular)
N.Y.K.
N.C.948.
TOKYO - 11th Apr11 1934.
Dear Sirs:
NEW PHILIPPI E/U.S. GULF PORTS SERVICE.
As a way to utilize the present fleet of our New
York Line, which is now to be improved by replacing
8 old vessels by the six new modern motor vessels, as
per our letter uated 4th Airil, we have decided to
inaugurate, as from Se tember of this year, the new
service from Philip ine ports to U.S.Gulf p rts, callin
at Havana :nd two or three principal ports in the Car-
ibbean Sea.

one of the aost promising markets for Japanese commod-
ities, in future.

below, which attract most shipments from Japane ports,
will be selected as the regular ports of call of this
new line.

every other month, by the steamers to be comnissioned
to this new line, but not to call at this port with the
6 new motor vessels of the New York Line. Tnis is to

volved en route to New York from Panama.
New Orleans.
This port is the terminal of this line at the oppos-
ite side on the o tward voyage, but the route will be
extended to the Atlantic ports., i. e., New York, Boston,
Philadelphia, Baltimore, etc., for discharcing cargo, if
required.
Number of steamers to be commissioned:
Six (6) out of eight (8) vessels which are presently ply-
ing on our Orient-New York Line.
The names of steamers to be allotted to this line will be
advised later on.
Service.

and the Caribbean sea ports on their outward voyage.

"Booking Plan-
1. Tor Vessels, not calling at Havana and the
Caribbean Sea ports.
Outward.
We expect mainly to fill the space of the
vessels with Philippine cargoes, i.e., sugar,
cooomut oil in bulk, etc. destined to U. S. Gulf
ports and Atlantic Ports, if neoessary. (In
off-season of sugar in Philippine territory, we
shall be pleased to take lumber, homp, copra in
bulk, etc., for Pacific coast ports, Gulf ports
and Átlantic ports, if required.)
Homeward-
The basic cargoes will consist of cotton,
phosphate rock, scrap iron, etc. in the Gulf
ports and Florida.
11. For vessels calling at Havana and the Caribbean
Sea ports.
Outward-
Besides the cargoes from Philippine terri-
tory, we earnestly desire to book as much general
cargoes as possible, from Japan ports for U. S.
Gulf ports, llavana and the Caribbean sea ports.
STRICTLY
Homeward.
The principal cargo will be cotton, phos-
phate rook, scrap iron, etc., from Gulf ports and
Florida, as mentioned above.
Schedule-
As soon as we can fix the schedule of this line
we will advise rou of it.
We sincerely hope tnat this new line will be an out-
standing success, thanks to your strenuous cooperation
and efforts.
Trusting this will have your special attention, and
thanking you in advance, we beg to remain,
Yours faithfully,
N.Y.K. Mgr.Frgt.Dept."

was received from Captain M. Pinto Bazurco, Peruvian Navy,
a translation of the document is transcribed herewith.

Pursuant to what Captain Pinto Basurco states about
there being numerous Japanese in this district with mili-
tary or naval training, there is most likely something in

family in Miraflores, which, if true, might tend to bear
this out. It appears that the finily in question had a
Japanese mayordomo (butler), who was in the habit of leav-
ing the house before daylignt every morning for about two
hours, ostensibly to gather herbs in order to cure some
STTRICTY
malady with which he was afflicted. The owner of the house
claims to have forced his way into the mayordomo's room
during one of these excursions and stated that he found a
good library in French, German, English and Spanish, all
dealing with mapping, surveying and engineering subje cts,
i.e., books which are not ordinarily found in possession
of domestic help. The mayordomo, it is understood, stated
that he had been working on a map or plan of the entire Mira-
flores district, and as soon as the owner of the house men-
tioned the subject the mayordomo resigned immediately. He
sovered hims elf by explaining that he was a student of civil
engineering.
In short, there are a number of such tales floating about,
and hearing them is merely a question of keeping one's ears
open. It is possible, however, as they are so frequently
heard, that there may be:a certain amount of foundation for
many of such rumors.
IMMIGRATION.

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
- 31 -
IMMIGRATION.
With respect to the history of Japanese immigration
in Peru, Vice Consul McKee has gathered considerable in-
formation on this subject, which was submitted in his
report entitled:"Trend of Migratory Movements in Peru",
dated August 29, 1934, a copy of which was forwarded to
the Embassy. A brief summary, therefore, will furnish
an idea of the Japanese immigration in the Republic, and
the number of Japanese nationals residing here.
Inasmuch as the Japanese immigrants did not at first
affect Peruvian labor to any great extent, no restriction
measures or exclusion laws were enacted against them. In
u!
1897, the admission of 1,200 Japanese was the first hint of
any Nipponese "penetration", and caused serious riots on
the part of the plantation laborers in the coastal valleys
where they were employed. A few years later, several hun-
dred Japanese laborers were brought over to work in the
rubber plantations in the Madre de Dios Valley in south-
eastern Peru. When the depression in the rubber industry
became severe, these colonists turned to agriculture and
migrated to towns, resorting to the operation of small stores.
In 1919, a tract of some 800,000 hectares of mountain land
was said to have been acquired by certain Japenese inter-
ests in the Huanuco district, and it is stated that only
a few Japanese colonists h&ve ever taken advantage of it,
and that very few Japanese have been introduced to colon-
1ze it.
Vice Consul Forsyth, who was recently on a vacation in
the Huanuco, San Ramon, La Merced and Chanchamayo districts,
states that he saw little or no evidence of Japanese en-
gaged

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
-32 -
gaged in agriculture in that sector of the montana, and
that the bulk of them appear to be engaged in the
"pulperfa" (saloon-store), hotel, parber shop, hot el-bar and
café bus iness.
Mr. McKee, in the above-mentioned report, states that
a new law is being drafted in the Peruvian Congress to
exclude Japanese and Chinese immi rants from coming to
Peru. Also, it is stated that the Ministerio de Fomento
is arafting a law to limit the entry of Japanese into Peru
and that this is scheduled to be presented during the re xt
session of the Peruvian Congress. This, however, is hard-
ly in accordance with what one of the Peruvian immigration
officers told me on board the other day. He states that
the Japanese were "Completamente afuera de la ley", i. e.,
that they were not hindered by uny legal restrictions what-
soever as long as they ill not become a public charge. The
Ministerio de Fomento states that its recoras indicate that
there were approximately 12,00 Japanese residing in Peru
in 1925; over 18,000 in 1929; near1y 21,000 in 1931, and
about 23,000 in 1934. rhe number of Ja anese, counting
women and Peruvian-born children at present residing in
Peru is estimated by the Lima Chamber of Comaerce as being
44,oo0. As to the control exercised over the Japanese at
the present time, it is understood that these nationals are
excluded by treaty exemption from the regulations pertain-
ing to the registration in the Sección de Extrangeria.
Some time ago, in diocussing immigration in general,
with Sr. Ramon Barreto, Jefe de la Seccion de Extrangeria,
he stated that the number of Japanese in the Lima-Callao
district

JAPANESE BUSINESS N LIMA.
In the provinces, apart from the agricultural activ-
ities, the commerce in wnich the Japanese are engaged is
generally of a petty nature, and confined to quantity and
not quality. Japanese can make a profit in a business in
whioh a Peruvian could not, and if a Peruvian could con-
duct

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
- 34 -
duct such business the Japanese compe tition would be
keen, even ruinous. The scope of activities of the
Japanese in Lima, nowever, is broader, and there are
several large Japanese firms engaged in manufacturing
on a relatively large scale, and also firmly entrenched
i the department store enterprises, handling textiles,
clothing, novelties, et cetera. A glance through Lima's
husiness directory Dears this out.
For instance, of the ninety-odd stores wnich are the
nearest appro:ch in Lima to a general or department store,
nearly twenty-five are shown under Japenese names, and
among the latter are included some of the largest organ-
izations of this nature in the city. Seventeen of Lima's
forty-one hotels are operated by Japanese. There are
nearly four hundred and twenty Japanese " ulperfas" and
"encomenderias", i.e., small retail rocery stores in
Lima, and of the two lhundred and sixty bars, cafes and
canteens registered in the city, two hundred and nine are
listed under Japanese names.
This is by no means a conclusive study of the extent
to which these nat ionals have entered into the city's com-
merce, but merely furnishes an idea of the magnitude of
the Japanese penetration of this region. Moreover, the
infiltration into the realm of petty commerce is increas-
ing steadily, particularly in the sale of quick turnover,
Japanese-manufactured articles, sold at low prices. The
bulk of these goods, due to their Japanese origin, move
in Japanese vessels, and, of course, have a material bear-
ing on tonnage carried in American ships, and furnish stiff
competition to American goods as well.
AGRICULTURE.

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.97)
- 35 -
AGRICULTURE.
The problen. whicn is probably the most serious at
the present time at least is the invasion of the Japan-
ese on the good agricultural lands in Peru, and it is a
feature which is causing serious anxiety among the Peruv-
ian agriculturists. We have discussed this subjeot with
several prominent agriculturalists and there is a note
of alarm sounded ev ry time this matter is mentioned.
It appears that the Ja anese are obtaining control
CCONNO
of the land in the neighboring valleys, i. e., Lima,
Chillon, Huacho, Chancay and Cariete, in ever-increasing
quantities. From such information as this office has
been able to gather, the Chancay Valley is, both in busi-
ness and agriculture, lmost one hundred percent Japanese.
A fair proportion of the good cotton land of the valley
is owned outright by them and the rest is said to be leased
or rented on long tern (ten and fifteen year) leases. They
are said to be endeavoring to obtain control of the land
in the Lurin Valley in the same manner. The Chillon Val-
ley, from the upper boundary of the Hacienda "Naranjal",
is stated to oe farmed almost entirely by Japs. In Huacho,
Supe and Cagete, there are a few Japanese, but the greater
part of this land is farmed at present by Peruvians. I
understand that north of Supe and south of Canete, there
are practically no Japanese engaged in agriculture, and
that they confine their interests strictly to the petty
commerce and"pulperfa" (saloon and general store) business.
According to local agriculturalists the thing that wor-
ries them more than any other feature of this "invasion",
is that the Japanese, in leasing their lands, usually pay
about

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.97)
- 36 -
30 to 50 percent more than the land is actually worth,
and in many cases more than can even se gotten out of
the land in a normal or better than average cotton year.
They apparently utterly disregard the economic value of
the land and seem to ignore that one should take into
consideration what land will produce. In one or two
cases, investigations have been mude by Peruvian agricul-
turists as to the productivity of lands farmed in this
manner by the Japanese, and these proved that the Japan-
ese farmers had lost money on tneir cotton during a good
cotton year, and yet continue to renew their leases at
the same rental or lease rate.
Their object, or reason
for doing so, is inexplicable.
ATTTILA
In spite of newspaper attacks on the landowners for
renting to the Japanese at fantastic prices, the temper-
ment of the average Peruvian who controls a considerable
quantity of land, is not such that he is going to pass up
any such opportunity to rent nis propert,. This is par-
ticularly so, since the Japanese are sure pay, comply
punctually with their obligations, and often pay in advance
for a long term lease. The Peruvian "yanacon" or lessee,
on the other hand, takes his property on, let us say, a
ten-year lease, pays promptly for the first four or five
years, and then as often as not, gets absent minded about
paying his rent, and the owner, under Peruvian law, finds
it almost impossible to get him off the land, and some-
times finds it cheaper to even loan him money, with the
hope that the lessee can pull out of the financial rut
and eventually liquidate his indebtedness. Under these
circumstances, it is no trouble for the Japanese to get
his

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
-37 -
his hands on any quantity of first rate land.
There are some fairly wild tales about, concerning
the acquisition of these lands by Japanese agriculturists
and farmers.
One case which was brought to our attention,
was that of a prominent Lima landowner with property in
Lima and Caiete valleys, a Sr. Luna Iglesias. He stated
that he had about a hundrea and twenty fanegadas ( 1 fane-
gada - 7.4 acres) of cotton land, of which he planted
only twenty-three, because of bad prices and yield during
the previous year.
. His intentions were to let the land
rest, and although he had several excellent lease offers,
he refused them. On being approached by certain Japanese
interests he was offered nearly double what the land was
worth, on a long term lease, with a substantial payment
in advance. Naturally, he closed the deal with the Japan-
ese and would have veen unwise to do otherwise.
We have also heard, from a good source (but to be taken
for what is is worth), that every time a Japanese rents
a piece of property he asks the owner of the land for a
legalized "constancia" (certificate) that he is in poss-
sion of the land for a specified lengtn of time. It
seems that he takes this official paper to the Japanese
Consulate General in Lima, who,it is alleged, turns it
over to someone who brings out a Japanese, passage free,
to work the land with, or for the lessee. This alleged
practice was divulged to an hacendado (planter), who is
a personal friend of the staff of this office, by a Japan-
ese mechanic and blacksmith who makes his living by re-
pairing tractors and farn equipment for various cotton
planters in the Lurin Valley. Moreover, this method of
importing

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
-38-
importing Japanese farn immi rants appears to be common
gossip in that region, and true or not, the Japanese in
that valley openly make a practice of farming their
leased lands with people of their own nationality.
This same planter has stated that in the leasing of
the farm lands, if the Japanese do ot have the capital
to pay for the turms of the lease outright, they go to
the Japanese Consulate General in Lima Where they are
placed in touch with some Japanese wno guarantee payment
of the rent. The informant states that he is endeavoring
to verify whether this is actually done by the Japanese
Consulate General or whether the Japanese Consul General
arranges guaranteed payment on behalf of some sort of
Japanese cooperative society which acts as a "land bank".
We have since been given to understand that there is a
strong Japanese cooperative society which is organized to
handle agricultural loans, and which finances these nation-
als in such transactions. It is hardly believable that
the Japanese Consulate General in Lima would openly indulge
in any such transactions or that the Japunese Government
would countenance a practice of this nature, other than
perhaps to confirm that such a cooperative society as may
exist, would be in a position financially to guarantee
payment to the owner of the land.
Apart from these unconfirmed rumors and statements, how-
ever, the agricultural situation is admittedly seriaus.
It is a proven fact that the land is steadily drifting into
Japanese hands, and that the local planters and farmers
are extremely annoyed and worried about it, particularly
about the fact that they can apparently do nothing to stop
1t.

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.98)
-39 -
it. They are now endeavoring, through articles pub-
lished in the local pross, to levelop sentiment against
the Orientals. An agrarian society is being formed, which
althougn it may nave a presidential candidacy in view,
intends to make a strong issue of the Japanese question
and to stamp it out by means of legal measures. This
thoroughly sensible action is being backed by a group of
farmers, who expect to obtain adherents numbering more
than 17,000. The organization is publishing its own
newspaper, "La Tierra", and is already vigorously after
the Japanese.
NEWSPAPER ATTACKS.
Practically all of the local newspapers, with the ex-
ception of "El Conercio" of Lima, have on various occas-
ions, published articles and editorials, directed against
STRRICTLY
the Japanese "invasion" of Peru, nd the manner in which
these Orientals are obtaining control of the agriculture
and petty commerce of Peru. A local cotton planter, started
what amounts almost to a campaign by publishing an expos-
ition of the rural situation, in "La Cronica" of Lima, and
various other papers. He tells us that "El Comercio" re-
fused to publish his article because the largest advertising
accounts of that paper are those of the Japanese firms in
Lima. The paper, consequently, did not wish to run the risk
of losing this business.
These newspaper attacks are all written in the same
tone, and refer to the agricultural peril as well as point
out the extent of the infiltration of Japanese into Peru.
They emphasize the fact that this has all been done so
quietly and so gradually that the Peruvian people have not
realized

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
-40-
realized the alarming extent of this influx of orientals
until it is almost too late to take remedial measures.
For the most part, such puolished articles, nave been fol-
lowed by favorable editorials. Incidentally, the press
campaign, in so far as it refors to agriculture, was orig-
inally started by a young American plantation owner, Mr.
Charles A. Luamis, and his remarks have, without exception,
receivei warm su port on the part of the Peruvian agri-
culturists. The facts stated by ni. have never been re-
futed in the public press and no one has challenged his
remarks or even pointed out that he is a foreigner and
not ieruvian, but on the contrary, he nas received raise
L1
from numerous Peruvian sources, published editorially
2
and otherwise, approving his statements.

article, which indicates the general tenor of the press
on this subject:

"IN DEFENSE OF THE NATIOHAL AGRI-
CULTURE.
"Sr. Director de 'La Cronica'.
"At the present time, the entire world suf fers
the effects of a growing Japanese competition in
the different oranches of commerce and industry;
and as a result of which many nations have been
obliged to adopt protective measures against this
ruinous competition. Durin the past few months
we have seen in Peru the representatives of the
local textile industry uniting in order to take
protective measures against the grave threat of
the importation of Japanese manufactured products
whose prices are extremely low, due to infamous
wages paid to Japanese workers, and to the low
standards of living of the latter.
"What might be called a 'peaceful invasion of
Peru' is now present in a very intense form, and
is offering a serious problem in its four aspects;
namely, economical, political, religious and social;
but among these, there is a more grave and immediate
danger, whose damaging consequences are being felt
today:

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
- 41 -
to-day: the increas ing absorption of the agri-
cultural lands of the coast by Japanese subjeots.
There are valleys such as Chancay and Huacho, for
example, which appear to be cenuine Nipponese col-
onies, as a result of the manner in which the land
and conmerce have been taken over. A reat part
of the Lima valley is in the nands of Japanese
agriculturists (?) and the same situation is occur-
ring in many other valleys on this coast.
"I do not believe that the Peruvian coast is so
in need of inhabitants that it must, as might be the
case in the 'montaia', import  eople from the oppos-
ite side of tne world to colonize its lands, thereby
depriving the national labor element of the right
of working its natal round. secause it is n unde-
niable fact that where a Jap nese plows, sows, culti-

that it is superfluous to  lan u e irrigation pro-
jects and at the sa.e time p sively er it the best
lands now under ultivation, and wit abundant vater,
to pass into unproductive hands. And I say unproduct-
ive, uecause the wo kin method of the Ja anese is such
that they sustain themse ves with the little they plant
of articles of rime necessity, and wnat little that
they lack, is purchased fro. their co-nationals. The
product of their crops, once their rent is paid, they
send outside of te country, in such a manner that apart
from what is left to the ,ro iotor of the lands is
rent, and of the little that they pay to the Treasury
in taxes, the economic structure of the country means
absolutely nothing to them. This is the e uivalent of
a genuine alienation of the richest agricultural zones
of this coast.
"With respect to the social aspect the 'peaceful in-
vasion of Peru' by Japanese subjects, presents another
grave problem. It is a known fact that once in possession
of land, the Japanese exploits it without employing, un-
less absolutely necessary, any national (Peruvian) labor.
To arrive at this, he counts upon the aid and assistance
of his countrymen. 'he result is naturally an increase
in unemployment and poverty among the Peruvian field
laborers.
"Not only the tillers of the soil and the economic
structure of the nation suffer the effects of this ab-
sorption of the lands by Japanese, but all and every
social class - the cost of living tends to increase
greatly, due to this insane desire to take possession
of the lands under any conditions, whereby the Japanese
compromise themselves to pay their rent in cotton. The
planting of cotton is the only thing which interests
them. For this reason, to-day, an alarming scaroity
of the articles of prime necessity has been produced
with the consequent increase in prices. Tomorrow, the
soarcity will be more aggravated, and it may be readily
seen that in the vicinity of Lima one encounters almost
nothing but cotton; the minimum and inadequate propor-
tion of foodstuffs is not sufficient, in spite of the
fact

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.99)
- 42 -
"fact that there exists a law obliging the farms
to sow at least fifteen percent of their extension
with these products, and which, nevertheless, is
never complied with. Already, voices of alarm have
been heard, protesting against the unjustifiable
increase of prices of articles of prime necessity,
but it seems that no one has given proper attention
to the true origin of this increase.
"The method of procedure of the Japanese, in
taking a farm, or part of it, whether as renters or
'yanacones' (tenant farmers) i.e., offering to pay
transfer or rent completely out of relation with
the economic value of the land, renders useless an
offer of any other person who thinks of taking lands
to work with national workers and to take from the
land a reasorable interest on capital invested, con-
sidering the nature of the business and tne risks run
therein. And certainly, the national 'yanacon' can-
not nonestly and with the intention of complying with
nis obliations, compete with the Japunese, who does
not see the matter from n economic point of view.
The result of this is that an ever-inereasing number
of farms are almost entirely in the hands of Japanese,
and if there are among them Peruvian 'yanacones', the
only t ings that remain for the latter are unproduct-
ive and hilly lands.
"For this, the owners and renters of the lands may
be blamed. Many of them say 'What do you want me to
do, if the Japanese pay more, and comply more punctually
with their obligations?• If the Peruvian 'yanacones'
could count upon the same ca ital, and the same cooper-
ation that the Japan se receive, the former could comply
with their compromises. equally well or better than
the Japanese. In any event, there can be no reason suf-
ficiently powerful to justify the preference accorded
to people who come from the other side of the globe,
who invest no new capital, create no irrigation pro-
jects or fountains of wealth, but merely remove the bands
and the right to work them from the Peruvian farm worker.
We must remember that the agriculture in Peru has exist-
ed and prospered during many centuries without the as-
sistance of the Japanese.
"We have noted the dongers offered by colonization
of the coastal agricultural zones by subjects of Japan,
and those which are continually taking on a more alarm-
ing character, increasing each year at least eighty per-
cent, those lands which are passing into the hands of
this element. It remains to be seen what measures can
be taken before it is too late, to alleviate these dis-
astrous effects. Perhaps a law would be sufficient,
which would oblige all landowners or proprietors who
desire tenant farmers, to deliver at least eighty per-
cent of the extension to be tenanted to the Peruvian
element, and thus oblige anyone who works a piece of
land in excess of one fanegada (7.4 acres), to employ
a minimum of eighty percent national laborers. A law
of

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-43-
"of this nature would be the (natural) complement
of those already in existence: and in this manner,
without undue bad influence, could put a stop to
the xisting state of affairs of whose gravity no
one seems to be cognizant, for the future of the
country.
"I recognize, Sr. Director, that I have given
an opinion on a subject which is purely Peruvian -
and more - I have done it impulsively, for the
affection inspired in me for Peru, and for sincere
Pan American sentiment. I would appreciate any
space hich your usteemed daily might wish to give
this article, and I remain, with all consideration,
Your obedient servant,
C.A. LUMMIS.
Lima, 28th of June, 1934.

of the Japan-se daily newspaper LIMA NIPPO, which is

ese colony throughout Peru. It might be added that there

under the name of PEJIHO, which is said to have a more
limited circulation, copy of which is not available at
present as its circulation is very restricted.

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL.
NOTES ON JAPANESE ACPIVITIES
IN PRU.
INTRODUCTION.
Referring to the Embassy's reguest that this Consul-
ate General investigate certain features of Japanese
WILN
activities (immiration and com erce) in Pru, in accord-
ance with the instrution received by the Embassy from
D
the Department of State, the follo ing notes have been
3
TCTN
compiled along the lines suggested.
Much of the information herewith has been confirmed
but such of it as has not been confirmed, has come in the
majority of cases from what are considered good so urces,
C
and is included herein.
α
The information which was receive: in the bepartment,
TS
apparently paints an alarming picture of the Japanese sit-
uation in Peru. However, it would seem, as a result of
comparatively thorough inquiry that while the ingression
of Japanese into this country crcates dangerous situation,
at least at th present time the danger would appear to
lie in entirely different channels than those ment ioned
in the communication which reached the War Department.
The serious aspect at the present time, as we see it, con-
dists principally in a steadily augmenting penetration of
Japanese into agriculture and the petty commerce of Peru,
the absorbing of the country's fine agricultural lands by
these nationals, and the apparent inertia on the part of
the Peruvian Government to apply restrictions which would
limit the Japanese in their increasing activities. The
only recent overt restriction placed against foreigners is
the

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the 80 percent-national requiremnt, but t is appears to
be ineffective to cope with the Japanese infiltration.
CHIMBOTE.
In a communication which reache the War Department
from a private s urce, en informant stre sed the possi-
bility, or the probability of the Japanese taking over
the Peruvian port of Chimbote as eith r a naval base or
as
a harbor. The impression iven in the above-mentioned
CONEO
communication was that the enti re himbote distric t was
popul ted principally by Japanese, and hinted that these
were more or less masquerading under the occupations of
farmers, laborers and business men. An effort has been
made to confirm this, and inquiries have been made of con-
tacts who are entirely familiar with the Chimbote district,
and in some cases, with tnose who have interests there.
The only information which we have thus far been able to
gather, leads us to believe that the information alleging
Chimbote to be a Japanese base is exaggerated.
It is the understanding of this office that most of the
Japanese in the Chimbote district are small business people,
owners of barber shops, otels, small stores, "ohinganas",
pulperias", bars a d cafés, et cetera. It is stated that
roughly s ixty per cent of the small business in the port
is in the hands of the Japanese. This is also understood
to hold tmue at the port of Samanco, t he neighboring town
six miles to the southward of Chimbote. The entire popula-
tion of Chimbote and its immediate vicinity would hardly
exeeed two t housand, of which, we are told, between fifteen
and twenty percent are Japanese.
Chimbote

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
Chimbote is the outlet for the Janta Valley, an exten-
sive and fertile reglon, devoted largely to sugar and cotton.
The port is connected by rail with Taolones, 57 kilometers
up the valley, and Huallance, wi th an additional 81 kilo-
meters of railway line. There is also a 30-kilometer
branch line connecting the latter town with Chuquicara.
Approximately half of the Santa Valle is said to be-
lone to the Hacienda "Tnnbo cal", now in the nands of the
Banco Italiano and the Baneo Aleman ransatlantico, as a
E
result of mortgage foreclosures. Something over one-fourth
0
4
of the valley belongs to a large hacienda which formerly
belonged to members of either the Legufa or Vinces family,
but which we are told was confiscated and it is our under-
standing that it is now operated by locel interests. The
remaining section of the valley is owned and cultivated
by a few Peruvian inter sts having relatively lerge hold-
ings. From wher the Santa Valley extends i to the "Callejon
de Huayllas", this territory is farned almost in its entire-
ty by Peruvian lansowners. In short, such information as
this office has obtained, indicates that apart from the
petty com erce in the immediate vicinity of Chimbote ad
Samaneo, that the Japanese have not penetrated into the agri-
oulture to any alarming extent in the Chimbote district, and
there is no immediate danger of their doing so, according to
present indications.
The same conditions are said to exist in the Nepeña dis-
triot, a neighboring valley, which extends i land from Sam-
anco. Almost the entire valley belongs to the Lockett in-
terests, under the mme of Hacienda "San Jacinto", and the
ramaining,

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.95)
-4-
remaining, cultivable lands are held by Peruvians, with
few 1f any Japanese ongage: in a riculture. As is the case
with Chimbote, the stores, hotels, vars, barber shops, and
"chinganería" in general, in Samanco, are controlled prin-
cipally by the Japanese. urthermore, they appear to be the
only ones in that region who can make any oney in trading
with the poorer leruv on laooring classes. M. Kichard Lock-
ett informs us tuat ae formerly had about o0 Japanese La-
borers, but that for the past fift en years they have had
11
C
no oriuntal labor either at "San Jacinto or at the "Santa
Barbara" hacienda. The bulk or th se lautds are devoted to
NOU
sugar cultivation.
What has been stated about Chimoote being an exeellent
port is quite true. It is decidedly the best harbor in P、 ru,
and having seen most of the principal ports between the canal
U
Zone and Pue to Montt, Chil, it is probably one of the best
on the wost coast of South Ame ica. A description of the
harbor may enable those who have never seen it to obtain some
idea about it and its merits.
The bay is over six miles from north to south, recedes
about three miles, is well closed in, sheltered, with plenty
of water, and is easy of access. The principal entranee to
the harbor is one and three quarters miles in width, sounding
14 to 19 fathoms, i.e., ample water for any ocean-going ves-
sel. The bay itself sounds four and a half to ten fathoms,
with mud and mud-sand bottom, which offers good holding
ground. Barring two five-fathom patohes close in to shore,
the bay is free from all shoals and obstructions.
The harbor is closed in on $he northward by Mt. Chimbote
(on the mainlmd- altitude 1,640 feet); Blanca Island, ex-
tending in a northerly-southerly direotion one and thr ee-
quarters miles in length and having a high ridge ranging in
altitndo

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
altitude from about three hundred fifty to six hundred
sixty feet. To the southward of the passage, the Ferrol
Islands extend in a west-northwesterly direotion, and af-
ford ample protection against the southwesterly wind, swell
and current. The so thern boundary of the bay is formed by
Mt. Divi ion and a ridge (altitude - seven hundred eighty
to thirt on hundred tnirty feet) extendine out in a general
northwesteriy direction. this mo ntain and ri ige are con-
neeted with the mainland oy a low, sandspit.
We have flown
over this narbor and heve aloo had an opportunity to check
this information in the Inited States Hydroegraphic office
Coast Pilot and detail charts for the port, with Captain J.
Reznik, master of the American . . CCvDOR, as well as with
other.masters.
As stated in the enclosures to the instruction to the
Erbassy, the general physical, topo raphical and strategi-
cal set-up of the port of Chimbote, when viewed from the
angle of military or naval defense, is superb.
As a matter
of fact, only a short t ime ago a measure was suggedt d in
the Peruvian Congress, providing for the establishment of a
Peruvian Naval base at that port. The motion was brought up
in the session of Congress on Au ust 8, 1934, to recommend
that the port of chimbote be considered as a base for the
construct ion of a raving dock, and that a military and
naval base be established in the port.
It is possible that what may have prompted the allega-
tions made regarding Chimbote with respect to the Japanese
taking over Chimbote, was a rumor whioh was being ciroulated
in Limm and Callao about eighteen months ago that certain
Japanese

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
Japanese interests (I believe it was Okura & Co., Ltd.,
of Tokyo, but am not certain) were negotiating to lease
the port for a hundred years. Accoraing to the rumor, a
small port works was to have been constructed, the lessees
of the port planned to exploit coal fields in the interior.
The rumor, however, died a natural  eath, and apparently
nothing nas come of it. it reuchen this o fice about a year
or so ago through an Auerican living in the Chimbote dis-
prict, connected with minin: interests, and it is possibly
from this individual that e great part of the misinformation
about Cnimbote has been derived.
As the so urce of the in-
formation was known to tuis offi e as unreliable, the alle-
gations had alruady been dis carded as not well founded, or
rather, exaggerated.
SHIPPING.
With respeot to the exist ing Japanese steamship services
on this coast, the only regular line oporatine a scheduled
service between Peru and the urient is the Nippon Yusen Kaisha
of Tokyo. This line operates three comparatively large ves-
sels, one of which is about the last word in up-to-date oper-
ating and cargo handling vessels on this coast. the three
vessels in the trade are the M.S. HEIYO MARU (the newest of
this fleet), and the steamers RAKUYO MARU, and BOKUYO MARU.
Outward ports of oall (soheduled) are Hongkong, Moji, Kobe,
Yokohama, Honolulu, Hilo, San Francisco, Los Angeles, Manzan-
illo, La Libertad, Balboa, Buenaventura, Callao, Pisco, Mol-
londo, Arica, Iquique and Valparaiso. The homeward bound tri p
schedules calls at Iquique, Callao, Balboa, Los Angeles, Part-
land, Yokoaama, Moji and Hongkong. These vessels also touch
at Peruvian ootton ports northbound, if sufficient cargo of-
fers.
Vessels

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.97)
- 7 -
Vessels of this Japanese line we understand, are now
diseharging from two to four hundred weight tons of in-
bound cargo per voyage, or say, a rough double of those
figures, in so far as revenue tonnage is concermed.
For
a time the Nippon Yusen Kaisha vessels ran so light that
they were showing six to eight feet of water line and it
is frankly a mystery as to how the line managed to keep
them running, even with the strong subsidy. As to the
question of Japanese subsidizati on, we have peen unable
to le arn much accurate information, out recall from an
article published some time ago i the New York Marine
CNOC
Journal that Japanese ships ran: with the most heavily sub-
sidized shipping in the world.
The ainstay of the Japanese trade on the Peruvian
coast, however, has been, up until the past year or eighteen
months, compriseu largely of third class passenger business,
of which there is a fair amount even since the Chinese boy-
cott of the N. Y. K. Line. This boycott has existed almost
since the beginning of the Manchukuo seizure. while this
is apart from the present report it might be wor th mention
that there is a quarter-cast Englishman here, working in
conjunetion with the Dollar Line.
This individual speaks Chinese, is probably part Chinese,
and is in close contact with the colony. It is understood
that he is sapping both the N.Y.K.'s passenger and freight
business, which formerly moved on the Japanese ve sels, and
has been turned, to a great extent through other channels,
transshipping via the Dollar Line at Panama. This has
meant the loss of lucrative trade to the Nippon Yusen Kaisha.

"AMERICAN

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.98)

"AMERICAN CONTINNTAL
POLICY.
"10. All of the peoples of Amorica snoula take into
consideration the seriousnes8 of the Japanese peril,
which has now passed from the category of a simple threat
to an aggressive reality, as is evidenced by the follow-
ing: the nature of the Nipponese penetration into Peru;
the cynical attempt of Japan in its endeavor to obtain
possession of the Galapagos Archipelago - already brought
out in relation to the Colo bian-Peruvian incident - and t.e
fact that at the present time there are over z0o,0o0 men
of that nationality in S th Amerlca, with military in-
struction, and among whom are a proportionate number of
naval, army and aviation experts of various ranks of
chiefs, offi ers, sub-o fi :ers and non-co mi sioned pe r-
sonnel.
"It has recently been stateu in the United States
that the possibility sh uld be considered, of a Japanese
invasion by way of the Aleutian Islands and the Alaskan
Peninsula, but it a oears that there is also sufficient
foundation to consider the actual existance of the nuclei
which may easily be transformed into bases of concentra-
tion in or er to con rt Couth America into a formidable
bridge of assault with wien the Japanese forces would try
to annihilate the defundin forces of North American terri
tory.
"It is puerile to think that the United tates is
capable of respondin by itself, for the ocurity and the
defense of the American continent, and for that matter,
even the defense of its own soil, in the case of a Japan-
ese aggression. The epoch has now pesed when it was only
necessary to defend the Panama Canal. To ay the Zone lacks,
politically, the explanation, inasmuch as its defense has
to depend qqually upon North and South America. A power-
ful enough reason which would suggest tuat the sovereignty
of the Republic of Panama, su stitute i t elf for that of
the United States in the territory of the Zone, iving the
defense of the latter to both Amoricas, and the retention
of the economic administration of the Canal by the United
States.
"The defense of the Panama Canal obliges that the pro-
teotion of the Galapagos Archipelago not be overlooked, due
to the danger that it might fall into the hands of the
Asiatic invador, who would convert it into an advance
point in the system of attack on the Panama Canal. And
as Amerioa has not the right to hope that Ecuador will at-
tend to this adeguate protection by itself - the nations
whioh look out upon the Pacific might well look after this -
and especially Panama, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Bolivia and
Chile, coming to an agreement to effect an economic and
military administration of the said islands.
"2o. The strategio position of the Malvinas Islands (Falk-
lands) gives them an important role in the plan of defense
of the American continent, of which Canada forms a part,
and whose fate cannot be indifferent to its metropoli.
Nor can Eagland view with indifference, Australia and New
Zealand whieh are threatened with the same danger as America.
It is resultant, then, that the future of these three great
British nations must weight desisively, to end the English
ocoupation

=== Page 432 === (confidence 0.98)
- 20 -
"Oscupation of the Malvinas Islands, and that Argem-
sima enter into possession of the rights and duties
whieh its sovereignty indicates in this arohipelago.
". The coneurrenee of Bolivia in the adminimtra-
tion of the Galapagos and in the defense of the Pee-
ifie, harmonizes with its neoessity for an outlet
upon this (the Pacifio) sea. This could be obtained
in the form of one of the coves situated immediately
south of Arica, and take advantage of it commroial-
ly, as if it were her own property. There is no
doubt but what Chile would gain by this triangular
operation as by relinquishing its rignts over this
zone whioh it now retains, dvantages of all olasses
would compensate it.
"40. The conflicts Jividing the peoples of Amerioa
are a permanent obstacle for the prosperity of the
continental policy which it convenes them to follow,
and more, that with relation to this, though they
do not pass out of the oategory of simple domestio
quarrels, 1t costs much work to subordinate the,
oven to the point of getting them to consider that
America is the secret of the future of humanity, and
that the pooples who inhabit it cannot pay traitor
to the high, oultural mission with which they are
oalled upon to comply. It might well be that an out-
let from Bolivia to the Pacific oould open the road
to the termination of the dispute of the Chaoo.
"In so far as the conflict of the Amazon is con-
cerned, there are probably few who 1gnore that if Peru
signed the Salomon-Lozano truaty, it was due to the
prossure of the A erican govern ent, perhaps interested
in giving Colombia compensation which would file down
the roughness and brusqueness witu which it treated
that country in the noisy business of the Isthmus.
It was a bad deal for Peru, the signing of the treaty,
whieh could it have served the continental interests
when it was realized, would have proven itself now
that the cont inental situation hus changed. The neo-
essity of conserving it intangible subsists, above all
when there is so muoh to induoe one to think that a
Colombian-Peruvian war would only benefit Japan, whose
bankers, it soems, are waiting the opport une moment in
Limm to begin funotioning, and whose military mission,
contracted, it is said, by the former Peruvian Govern-
ment - it is not known for whet reason, is vaiting in
the capital.
Lima, May, 1934."
(Signed) M. Pinto Basuroo."

=== Page 455 === (confidence 0.99)
1osure 2 to Despatch No. 4007 of June 22, 1935, from the
Embassy at Lima.
Translation:
Lima, June 18, 1935.
Mr. Secretary of the American Embassy,
Lima.
Mr. Secretary:
▲ Peruvian citizen desires to give you the following
written information - something very interesting for you
(Americans) and also for us, South Americans.
I have a Japanese friend with whom I sometimes have a
drink or beer; and due to our longstanding friendly relations
or to the efrects of the beer, his tongue was loosened. the
Japanese, who claimed to be a former member of the Japanese
Army, said:
"Japan is an intelligent, disoiplined and warlike nation;
hence we can divide China, thereby forming new states, which,
under our wise leadership, we can convert into our obedient
subjeots who love us as we love our Japan. That is to say,
we shall make or them and of ourselves, one great power and
attack Lurope; after having been victorious in a war with the
United States, taking possession of California in less time
than they think possible. The yellbow race has to dominate the
white race, and once we have done this, we shall acquire
South and North Amerioa, because this is the Continent in
which the Asiatio race will settle. Now we already have in
South Amerioa - Peru and Brazil - some millions of Japanese,
all of whom are military men, and we keep introducing more
people, methodically, so that in this way, at the opportune
moment, we can crush the United States. The white race is a
worn-out race; there is no understanding among themselves;
and we

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.98)
 2 -
and we shall take advantage of this in order to arm states,
such as Manchukuo, showing them the advantages of puting an
end to the white race. Japan at the head of these States
will be formidable and we will give the final blow to this
oursed white race, which has humiliated us for so many cen-
turies, and which we, in turn, have to humiliate (you must
take note that the writer hereof is white and that he took
everything as a joke). We hate the Americans like canibals
for the humiliations we have suffered from them. In order
to purify the world, it is necessary that the yellow race shall
overcome the white race. Each yellow must be a power. The
war with China is a mere formula, a pretext to subjugate them,
divide them, teach them and afterwards to tirow them against
the rest of the world if possible. We need South America for
ourselves. We besmiroh ourselves with the Monroe Doctrine:
'He who has force, has right'".
Now then, Mr. Secretary, I think that all Japanese are
exactly like this one. All have the same aim: Hatred for
the white raoe!
An admirer of the flag with stars and stripes salutes
you, Mr. Seoretary.
(Signed) ▲ Peruvian Citizen.

Subject: Japanese Activities Near Chimbote, Peru.
10.1

1211
The Secretary of Staté,
AU1
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to inform the Depertment that
20223/29
stories of Japanese expansion and the furtherance of
Japanese designs of every kind constantly reach the
Embassy. Most of them are unworthy of credence and
it has not seemed neoessary to pass them along to the
Department. I have, however, endeavored to be alert
to disoover the realities so that any information of
real value might be communicated to the authorities
of our Government.
The Seventh Day Adventists in Peru are carrying
F/FG.
on a quiet and effective missionary work, particularly
in the outlying wild and savage portions of the country
and

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
and the Adventists' representatives go to every corner
of the nation and keep themselves well informed as to
what is going on.
They are friendly to the Embassy
and the President of the Mission especially has been
good enough to give the Embassy information from time
to time. As the Embassy has heard a good deal about
Japanese activity in the region of Chimbote, I asked
the President of the Mission, Mr. F. E. Bresee, on the
occasion of a recent conversation, if he would be good
enough to let me know the result of his observations on
his next trip to the Chimbote region. He has just re-
turned and under date of July 26th makes me the somewhat
1/ negative report which the Department will find herewith.
It seems to me that its very negetiveness is of value
in view of the extreme interpretation that has often been
given to Japanese activities at that spot.
I beg to suggest that our Navy Department may be
glad to see Mr. Bresee's memorandum.
Respectfully yours,
Fred Morris Dearing.

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.99)
molosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 4065, dated July 30. 1935,
from Embassy, Lima, Peru.
CONFIDENTIAL REPORT
JAPANESE ACTIVITIES IN AND AROUND CHIMBOTE, PERU
Professions
The majority of the Japanese in Chimbote are engaged
in small business enterprises, such as one finds in any
coast town of this country, which include restaurants,
barber shops, small dry goods stores, hardware and tin
shops, and, in some instances, grocery stores. There is
one Japanese who has a business of considerable proportion
of general merchandise, wholesale and retail. Very, very
few Japanese are located on acreages. In fact, I was
unable to find a single Japanese owner of any great portion
of land or lands. There is one oriental vwho has an
"hacienda" of considerable proportion, but he is a Chinese.
Buy or Rent
The majority of the Japanese business men rent the
buildings hich they occupy. The merchant who has the large
wholesale and retail business above mentioned is owner of
the real estate, including the buildings, which he occupies.
Value of Property Owned
It was impossible, of course, for me to ascertain,
other than by personal observation and calculation, regard-
ing the value of property owned by Japanese in and around
Chimbote, but I would place the figure at approximately
125,000.00 (soles).
Importation
There is considerable importation of dry goods, such
as silks, laoes, braid and the like, besides novelties
including

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
including articles and toys for children, lump shades and
electrical appliances.
It seems that there is an impor-
tation of a building cement, Japanese manufactured.
Number Coming In.
From all the information which I could gather regarding
this, there is very little immigration at the present time.
Present Number
From the information which I could gather and the ob-
servations which I could make, there are approximately
one hundred and fifty inhabitants in the Japanese colony
in and around Chimbote. I think tnis number is generous.
General Attitude
The general attitude of the Japenese in and around
Chimbote is not apparently ho tile or of a nature that
would cause suspicion. There seems to be no apparent
ambition on the part at least of those who are actually
there to dominate or command activities in that section.
True, they have a great portion of the small business and
at the same time it is difficult to know just what under-
current there may be. An oriental is always an oriental
and I believe that among the Japanese especially the cause
of expansion is ever prevalent, and this idea of expansion
is naturally no less manifest, visibly or invisibly,
among the Japanose of Chimbote.
F. E. Bresee, July 26, 1935.

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.99)
.1 to Despatoh No. 4371, dated January 15,1936,
from the Embassy, Lima, Peru.
A NORTE AMERICA
No hay tiempo que perder. Hay que trabajar por la union
de los pueblos de la raza blanca. La guerra en Europa, sería
un dosastre para la America entera. A los italianos hay que
dejarlos hacer. El bienestar de una nacion blanca y civili-
sada; está de acuerdo para el progreso del universo. A los
que hay que vijilar constantemente y bien de cerca, es á
los japoneses. Ellos se van como un rayo a un solo
objetivo: la América para los Asíaticos. os chinos se
oponen aparentemente a ser gobernados por ellos para engañar
al resto del mundo!! Es muy astuta esa raza!, pero a la larga,
ellos saben que salen ganando; Dirijidos por los japoneses
se vienen sobre America. Ellos ven en estas tierras un
paraiso terrenal. ¿Que hariamos con un ejercito aereo con
aero-planos baratos de bambu? Unos quinientos mil ... por
ejemplo, 50o,ooo. La palabra utopia, hay jue borrarla del
diccionario hoy en dia; todo es posible.
Estados Unidos debe tomar muy en serio esta cuestion,
Ustedes mejor que nadie saben que los juponeses tienen
hoy dia muchas islas repartidas en el oceuno, y que con-
stantemente llegan a ellos c: rgamentos de combustible para
sus aereoplanos.
Estados Unidos debe de upaciguar la
cuestion Europea y procurar de asestar un golpe mortal a
las pretenciones de los japoneses untes que sea tarde.
J. Vercellone
Lima, nero 1- de 1936.

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
losure No.2 to Despatch No. 4371, dated January 14, 1936,
from the Embassy, Lima, Peru.
(Tranlation)
TO NORTH AM RICA
There is no time to lose. We must work for the
union of the peoples of the white race. War in Europe would
be a disaster for all America.
The Itelians must be left
alone.
The welfare of a white and civilized nation is
in accordance with the progress of the universe. Those
who must be watched constantly and very closely are the
Japanese. They act like a flash with only one objective:
America for the Asiatics.
The Chinese apparently oppose
being governed by them in order to confuse the rest of
the world. This is a very astute race. But in the long
run they know they will win, directed by the Japanese
they will come upon America. They see in these countries
a paradise on earth.
What could ve do a ainst an aerial
army of cheap bamboo airplanes, some 500,000 for example.
The word Utopia must be erased from the dictionary hence-
forth. All is possible.
The United States must take this question very seriously.
You, better than any one else, now that the Japanese today
have many islands throughout the ocean and that cargoes of
fuel for their airplanes are constantly arriving. The
United States must settle the European question and find
the means of administering the death blow to the Japanese
pretensions before it is too late.
J. Vercellone

Japanese Take Over Thermal Baths.
An article in SUPLEMaNT0 of arch 12, 1936 deprecated
that the thermal baths of Churin had heen allowed to fall
into the hands of Japunese who are already charging ad-
mission for baths which hitherto had been free, notwith-
standing they have made no im prove nts in th estarlish-
ment.
